DIRTY EGGS NO USE.

_ A dean egg has a. mu& more attractive an,d appetising appearance than one-Which is dirt Yet this fact, which is apparentjy,.Ta obvious, is very frequently ignore& and theeggs are tient to maket dirty,„ pqentibly withismne nianwre,nctu- ally clinging to them. Rather than send a dirty egg to market it should be iviShd. Better than this, however, the nest 6 where the hens lay should be clean, for then the es -themselves will he clean. Washing should not be done if it can. be avoided, for there is as. much bloom on a new-laid egg .as there is on a grape, which Washing entirely removes: Apart from the im- proved appearance of a clean egg, there is an- other important consideration; The flavour of an egg is very easily and quickly affected, and it stands to reason, therefore, that if there is dung 'or other dirt sslhering to the shell for any longth of time it, is botind to injure the flavoqt.

GRADING AND PACKING. It is a great mistake, as well as of doubtful honesty, to place the large eggs4on the top of the basket and the small ones below. It may succeed once or twice, but it will soon be ills- covered, greatly to the detriment of the pro- ducer. All the very .large And -very smelt: eggs should be used at home. The former dwarf the others, making the ordinary sized ones appear small 3 the latter dissatisfy the purchaser. The foreigh eggs which arrive in this country are graded into three sizes, and only those, of ,one size pOked in each crate. A mark on the ont- side at once indicates to the purchaser the eiact size of the eggs within. This is an excellent plan and might .well be imitated in this coun- try. The eggs ,should: be .packed carefully, for, breakages retinae the 'profitV greetly. S4-eme of the more enterprising railway companies return empty egg boxes at a very small. charge, ad i thia,:ease the best way of nding egp to market is AL a well and substantially. made..box with felt or cardboard divisions. The eggs carry extremely Well 'in these boxes, a broken 41:!,P .being.. of very rare..,qc c rrenc e packing, and unpacking, too, are extremely sithiile arid expeditions.

Potato Omelet.

- boiled • mash

Two or three peeled potatoes, with one ounce -butter, add two ounces stale bread crumbs, and one well-beaten egg, a little milk,_ seasoning and little chopped parsley. Place in buttered dish, smooth the top, cover with thin layer of fine bread crumbs and a few piece of butter; bake in a brisk oven. . ,

Moulded Apples. Two pounds of apples, boiled to a -pulp in just enough water to keep them from burning ;. four ounces of sugar, one lemon, and one ounce of gelatine. When, the apples are ready add the gelatine previously soaked in a little cold water. Mix all well together, Put into a wet mould and leave until -quite told and' firm. erve :with whipped cream or thick custard.

, Substitute for Suet Pudding. ' Wash .two ounces of tapioca and cover with water, anct soak overnight. Drain before useing. Melt two ounces of butter and mix well with the tapioca. Place it in a bowl with_ three ounces of wholemeal floura,nd a little salt. Work flour and tapinca well together, place in. a but.: tered bowl or pudding cloth, and steam two hours.

Savoury PotatoeS. Cut up an onion and place at the bottom_ of a pie-dish; cut up about four potatoes,, then put a layer of them over the onion. Sprinkle a dessert-spoonful tapioca over them, add the re- maining potatoes, putting two ounces butter in pieces on the top, :Ida seasoning, fill the dish three yttrts full with boiling water, and cook in the oven; covered with a•cruat it is a potato pie; serve with one hard-boiled egg to ,each person. The Coming Vogue for White.

-, White will be very popular this spring and Summer ' in all kinds 'of - charming washing materials. The prevalence of almost universal mourning doubtless accounts for the chince of white .tbilettes, reliev.e4l.with touches of! black, worn affweddings and other functions th*t have taken place this month in London. But even thOse nut iti'morirning- will choose white gowns as the summer comes on. White duvettn is a favourite ,material for children's outdoor coats, :and for girls'in their early: teens it 'inak4s ideal coats and skirts, especially when wor# with white turs. The full skirts and the amount of .knitting &fie, may iccount for the refnrn of , the pocket, sometimes useful in size and shape. 'Mit not . uncoinmonly merely an ado7.ment. Those placed below the waist on fashionable .- ira,istco4tS ~-belfilig) to, i the Jattee o;tego y, but 1 they add a charm to this popular ac essory.

Those seen above the waist are sufficiently large to take a handkerchief or a small purse. On the new skirts the pockets are often exceedingly quaint, and very cleverly contrived with re- gard to their position.

The Vogue of the Waistcoat and the High Collar.

Bodices will be arranged this 'spring with sleeves of a contrasting colour, giving waist- coat effects; sometimes the waistcoat is of the Jockey type, fastening straight down the centre of the front; with , small buttons set close to- gether. One very original waistcoat was at- tached to the skirt Of the dress, with. which it was worn. Full in front, it consisted merely Of ; a pointed band •at the back. The Tabard bodice, that is such a, boon to tie home dress; Maker, has, been revived, and lends itself 'ad- inirably to the using up ot a short length of I)3rocade, velvet, or. face cloth. Laced under the arms, it show 'tlie- attiiily blouse' underneath, ~whilst a remnant of embroidery, or .coarse lace insertion makes in 'effective trimming. 'Collars 'get higher rather than lower, and the band of fur that has been used as a finish to the braided 'military type, .will. give way, when the weather, is warmer, to a pretty ruche, fastening in front. ;The latter is a great improvement.

A Game Lad.

Mrs. Nichol Longworth, at a dinner in Cincinnati, fold a quaint story about 'a- preco- Cious boy. "They, are very precocious, indeed," she said, " these • little 'chaps from Eton or Rugby, with their ,round, sober faces and their quiet air. A very pretty American girl was talk- ing one evening in London to one of these urchins. 'And have you got a sweetheart yet, Tommy?' she said,' playfully: No,'. , said Tannny; 'still, I'm game enough for a bit of spooning, if that's what you're after.' "

His Object. Most of us are acquainted with the person who asks obvious questiops7-the sort of pan who stops on in the middle of a headlong rush and asks you if you are in a hurry. Mr. E. is one of the pests, and during a 'Walk abroad the other morning he Paused in astonishment out- side a friend's house. Before it stood three huge moving vans; the lawn was almost covered with articles of furniture of Various sorts:— pictures, wardrobes, and china. And there was his old friend 8., begrimmed, weary and ill- tempered, directing operations in his shirt sleeves. .

"What ~ 8.," exclaimed Mr. E., moving?" "Not at all—not at all," snapped 8., with elaborate sarcasm, "Pin taking my furniture out for a ride!"

Useful Information in Popular Language.

The Future.

. _ A man's health belongs to his offspring ; he holds it 'in trust for their future use. The man who MisapprOpriates•tinistmoney held. for his children is Punished' as a'criminal. The man who squanders his health, which ought to have been safeguarded for ilke sake of his, chil- dren, should also. be punished. There are thousands of cases of defect, deformity and dis- ease which- 'owe ;their origin to the failure of the parents. • .:

Fresh Air.

• The main reliance in the cure of consumption is.fresh air, the wild variety that -grows out- doors, and is. kept out by those who -do not appreciate its value. In these days of well- made. houses, if one is reasonably careful it is possible to keep the same air in the house from Christmas to March, and make euse. of it in making the germs of consumption feel at home. In some parts'of the city. and Of tlio country as well, the air outdoors is exceedingly fin because people ..keep all the bad air shut up in the houses instead of letting it loose. That will: do if consumption is to be encouraged. If, on the other hand, it is to be fought, then let in the fresh outdoor air. Get it .in the daytime, .but especially make a point of having it at night. When peciple' come to see that it is a mark of ignorance,. to- '.say nothing about innate de- pravity,' to sleep with closed Windows, the war against the !.-white plague will be . assured of success. -

Bandages. , Bandages have often td be. improvised from handkerchiefs, 'cravats, Or rope, and in teach- ing first-aid we use Esmarch's triangular bmi- dage. ;Procure.a piece of calico some '4O inches square, and &at this diagonally •into two triangles. One of these may be folded to form the " broad bandage.," the " narrow bandage," or the "medium bandage." The, broad bandage is made;, by bringing the point of the triangle to th'e base and then the bandage is folded once. 'For 'the. marto* bandage the .broad handage folded once again, and the medinm bandage is mode_ by, bringing the .point of the triangle to the base and folding the bandage three times. The"• reef " knotis always used:in.first-aid, as the "granny "- -knot cannot be' depended- on. Hold the ends of the bandage in the tvio hands, and bring that held in the right over that in the left and under it; next bring the end in the left hand over that in the , right and' pass it through the loop. If the reef knot has. been properly tied, its free ends should lie in a .line with the bandage. .SPlints' are required to give _support and to, keep the broken ends of bone at rest., Sticks or.umbrella.s may bo used. lia.linorr- hage, if any, is attended 'to before the fracture, and a clean pad, is put over the wound -when the bleeding has been arrested; The- greatest pOssible care and gentleness must be observed.— `,` Nursing. Ilirror." . • • • • •

WHEAT COMING.

' The-American-wheat corner..may be regarded as broken. There are now over two million quarters on the way ,to England, and as, much to France, Italy and Ildlland., ..These supplies are so well ,protected,. despite Germany's -piratical bluster, that an insurance ,of 3.05. covers a ,thqo war risk. The wheat will arrive between now and the end of March, and by that , 'date.the large new crop of Argentina will "Unveil on its way here. The full tide.of Argentine ex- ports will run well into June, and India will have commented shipping in earnest, in May. , Thus the Americans have once again been a bit 'too cute, and will in. holding: out for 70s. be landed with heavy unsold reserves. The great increase in the American -area .of winter wheat will be supplemented in April bY a still grea,ter increase in Canadian Spring wheat. The ton- nage, crisis tontinues,, but when .`.`Kitche,n.er's , million!! are.once across the channel, together with., theirs stores,. this difficulty should in turn be greatly reduced.—" Standard."

AGRICULTURAL RESTORATION OF BELGIUM AND FRANCE.

The committee formed to 'afford -an opportu- nity to agriculturists, horticulturists and others in England and Wales of helping in the restora,

tion ot:the war-ravaged.,areas. of Belgium and North-Eastern France, when the way is open, is receiving wide and 'substantial sympathy and support. At the last meeting. of the Central Chamber of Agriculture a resolution was cor- dially -adopted pledging the Chamber to assiit this movement to the fullest extent, and stig- ge,sting thaf'Serbitt should' be Ineluded:in the scheme. Representatives were appointed on the central committee. Provincial Chambers and Agricultural SoCieties have been invited to form county committees. The Yorkshire Union of Agricultural Clubs .and Chambers has already taken step's'in that directioit, and is forming„sucli. a committee for .tika.k.great county. The Farmers' Club at its last meeting decided to support the scheme and 'elected its chairman as representative. Many other provincial socie- ties have not 'yet held their .meetings, and a large 'response is 'anticipated within a few days. A proposal is under consideration by one society that -a• contribution of £2OD shall be given to. this object. -Libral,,,promises of -ipp- port are being received. Among others nOt,pre- viously .aiiounced Messrs. :thtrtons, of ,War- ringtou....4rp, read to render help in the. Salit'e manner.; Offers have come to hand from other coUntries, and also gifts .Of stock and imple- ments.are offered. Contributions can. be sent to

the honorary treasurer, Agricultural Restora- tion Fund,. Six Robert Williams, .Bart., M.P., Williams Deacons Bank, 21-23,- Victoria-street, London, S,W.; Or to the honorary director; Mr. Edward Brown, 39,, Queen Anne's Chambers, Westminster, London, S.W.

THE PRICE OF MILK.

The subject ct the moment among dairy farmers and milksellers is the price of milk.. January closed with, a London average of IGd. a gallon at.the northern ,stations,,l.3d. at Liver- pool-street and .1.61 d. at Waterloo.: At. Birmin- gham 16d. was reached ,day after day, and at Manchester 15d: and 16d. The question is a serious one, and many country retailers are pay- ing as much as they get; and are still unwilling to raise the retairprice. HORSES IN THE WAR..

• A correspondent of the. "Times" '.deals with the excellent. work of Our Army Veterinary Corps in .F.ranee, and says. the British. horses -are the .admiration of everyone. ,The corps is quite a new department, established -.after the Boer War: It is the particular care of the Veterinary Corps to collect " wounded and aban- doned, horses belonging to ,both Armies. These horses are examined; the less seriously injured are-operated On .on the spot; while those which are badly wounded or are sick are sent away to a regular systeM of base hospitals. • -One veterinary :officer Was riding a, 'fine charger. which, had once belonged to a T,7lilan,' The horse had been found with a bullet in the shoulder. •A captain .of the corps, extracted' the ball literally under shell fire; with the result that after a few day's nursing the British Army

was richer by a fine horse. Hundreds of hor,e4 have been saved in this way, and the work of the. ;Army 'Veterinary.; Corps 'may haVe a, yery- itripOrtant bearing on the campaign. It _ham been cinnputed that every IMO and Freneh cavalryman has, on the 'average; ridden through three homes since the beginning of the War. MANUFACTURERS OPTIMISTIC FOR THE FUTURE

We are happy to say that local trade has, thus , far, not been very Seriously affected by the war, and much invaluable work has been undertaken for the Government. Large num- bers of. men have gone to the .front, and.the principal local firms have done their best to encourage this spirit of patriotism, and, in many instances, have arranged to supplement the pay of their employes whilst on active service and to keep .their places open. THE EXAMINER,

SATURDAY, FEBRUARY 27, 1915.

THE DIFFERENCE.

Now that the Warrington Territorials have gone to the Front, we have a greater personal interest than ever in the struggle that we and our Allies are engaged in for the freedom Of Belgium, the liberty of Europe, and the un- checked progress of civilisation. As LORD DERBY and other speakers at the real recruiting meeting on Wednesday night said, we have a splendid regiment in the South Lancashires, and a not less splendid Territorial Battalion of the same regiment, to keep in perfect trim, so far as a supply of suitable men can do it. But we have such reserve powers that besides seeing to the output by our works of legitimate Government re- quirements, will give to the Field some recruits for that indispensable section of our military forces, the Artillery, which .have long been the admiration and envy of the world. We. owe a great deal to LORD DERBY for his 'efforts ,in this great crisis, the remembrance of which should impel us to do our duty in pursuance of his powerful plea. He dealt with the question of National Ser- vice in the ghiSe of one who had told the people what would happen, and, in the light of a justified prophet, told us what would now happen unless we gave more, and yet more, men to. the colours. We can afford to wait awhile before we decide upon the merits or demerits of National Service. Both advocates and opponents would do well to let the question rest until we have the leisure and the peace of mind to consider it.. The war will have changed many things. Considerations will rule on both. sides that have been absent heretofore, and we must all be prepared —later on—to approach the matter of national defence with an open mind. There can be -no doubt, however, as to the urgent and present need for men, and we could not do better than repay LORD, DERBY by showing him that we are not only willing but most anxious to follow his great patriotic lead: as Mr. ARTHUR CROSFIELD urged his fellow- townsmen to (Jo. The war is taking its normal course, which is equivalent to saying that the Germans are getting more desperate than ever, and the prospect of the Allies is becOmihg more satisfactory. A con- trast or methods is about the best thing to use to shoW how and where we stand. Our Navy- has -effectually . bottled, up the Gerrrian Navy. There was a time when they ventured out to bombard ' fortreses as Scarbor-

ough, but the Lion and the Tiger bit the, raiders so seriously on the last-attempt that even Scarborough is now spared. But they have applied their system of " frightfulness " to peaceful' merchant- men. As their land forces herded peaceful villagers—men, women and children—in the public places of Bel- gian centres of population and turned their machine guns upon them with sickening effect, so they now have turned their submarines upon trading vessels of all nations that venture into the British waters that it has pleased them to term a war area. They will doubtless descend to any depths that their dark and desperate minds sug- gest. But the contrast we wish to point' out is this: That while they are wasting their time torpedoing undefended, helpless steamers, we and the French are busy, attacking the Dardenelles. the formidable lines of fortresses that have ' been looked upon with awe for generations. The Germans have- sunk or damaged six small ships in as many days-; We are aiming at the heart of a corruptcEmpire, and foreshadowing the expulsion of that Empire from civilised Europe.

On land we'are similarly- engaged in a serene, confident and business-like frame of mind. The enemy, by a great concentration of force, have ejected the Russians once again from East Prussia; but our Russian Allies have, once again, as they -will ever, do, turned at bay on their own lines .and robbed the enemy of that great victory which, n anticipation, they have already celebrated in Berlin. The plan , of Germany, ills believed, waslo strike down Russia and'then turn upon us in the West. Six months ago they were to strike us down and turn upon Russia. They did not strike us down, and they have not struck down' Russia. They are now weaker and we are infinitely stronger. The measure of our strength is in the fact that we are hammering at the mighty gates of Con- stantinople, while*the measure of their weakness lies in the fact that, simul- taneously, they dre enjoying themselves sinking a few coal and cotton boats.

===== 0002601_19150227_art0065.txt | included=true | included =====
INCREASING RATES AND RENTS.

The rates are to go up with a bound, and there is little doubt the rents will follow suit in sympathy. Estimates ofl the municipal expenditure for 191516, to be submitted to the meeting of the' Town Council next Tuesday night., will show that total rates amounting to 9s. 4d. in the £ will be necessary. As the rates are now only Bs. in the X the rise will be Is. 4d. in the £, the largest increase in Warrington on record. Whet' the Education Act came into operation, the rise was from 6s. 6d. to 7s. 4d., or a difference of tenpence, and it took till 191 . to reach the ,round Bs. at which it now stands. This last in- crease takes place, too, -simultaneously with an increased aSsessment, for Mr. W. H. WooncdcK, the-Assistant Over- seer, has produced a new Valuation list, which shows extra productiveness of nearly £3,000 a year, which is equal to a rate of 21-d. in ,the and makes the product of a penny rate £1,190. This is taking it both ways, and points, as we say, unmistakably to a rise in rents. There may be a means discovered on Tuesday of making savings and ,so lowering the increase somewhat, but

'

===== 0002601_19150227_art0066.txt | included=true | included =====
THE EXAMINER, SATURDAY, FEBRUARY 27, 1915.

hopes founded on that contingencyare not very strong when we 'consider the causes that have brought about the result. The great factor in the case is that the Gas Works, hit in severaltways by War conditions, will be able only to give £4,000 in relief of rates against' £13,000 last year, or a decline of £9,000; But ten or twelve years ago the Gas' ' Works were not giving 'anything in relief of rates; the Committee retained the profits for credit balances and the like. Against this, ten or tWelve-,years ago, education was costing the 'rates Only one penny in the ; next year it will cost £3,000 more than last year and call for a rate of a fraction over 2s. in. ilie X. This is money well spent, is a hard " fact in the rates. It has been decided tO give a war bonus to the employes of the Corporation according to their rate of pay, which will absorb £l,OOO and account for one of addi- tional pennies on the rates. The Over- seers and Guardians ask for nearly an additional penny. Thec there, are the normal increases in the expenditure of the Committees, which, in 'normal times, would have been .met ~by the increase in valuation. But 'we. are not living. in normal times, and We must brace ourselves to the task. of enduring till peace once again smiles upon us and our municipal affairs•:.

===== 0002601_19150227_art0067.txt | included=true | included =====
THE ROLL OF HONOUR.

Warringtonian itilled by a German

Sniper.

The roll of 'honour has been augmented by the death in the'firing 'line of Rifleman Edmund' Green, formerly a moular in the employ of Messrs. Fletcher, Russell and Co., who resided at Mersey View, 332, Knutsford-road. He was nearly 20" years of age. A fortnight ago on Monday, so' his comrades say, he was engaged in some trench work, and at 10.30 a.m. one of the enemy's snipers shot him through the head and death was instant. His comrades' carried 'hie body to the base, and after the usual military ceremony it was interred by the Mde of other comrades who had fallen in actiep. Early in the outbreak of the war, "Teddy," the name by which the deceased was best known to his rela- tives and friends, was actuated by the most patriotic of desires to do his best for his coun- try and joined the 6th Reserve Battalion of. the Rifle Brigade.

An Excellent Rifleman. Riflethan Green was an excellent shot, and in a letter to his mother shortly before Christmas he told her he was being drafted, along with some 28 old soldiers, to go to the Front with the First Battalion. He was then stationed at Sheerness. In the letter he told his mother and sister arid' brothers not to be downhearted and not to worry, for he would look after himself. He had hoped to spend Christmas at home, but going into France made it impossible for him to do so.

His last letter to his mother, dated January 31st, 1915, gives his address as "No. 2,067, Rifle- man E. Green, let Battalion Rifle Brigade, A Company, 2 Platoon, 4th Division, British Expeditionary Forces." After .acknowledging the receipt of cakes and letters from his mother and family, he mentioned the name of Harold Newall and a few other Warrington lads whom he left at Sheerness. He said that being in the firing line, they did not often get a chame'to write.

===== 0002601_19150227_art0068.txt | included=true | included =====
"Water Ran Over With Dead Bodies."

After a week's service in the line they were called into billet for three days in order to wash themselves and get "recruited up." While in the line they dare not wash themselves, because the water "ran over with dead bodies and all sorts of rubbish."

Continuing, he said our troops gave the Ger- mans a good dishing up on Thursday, for they blew a lot of their trenches and ammunition houses up, so that they had it a bit quieter the next day, and men were walking about in day- light in place of having to crawl about on hands and knees in the darkness. They had a rough time of it a fortnight previous; when eight of them were sent on what they call "snipers." Only four of them could get to their posts. The Germans had a gun trained on them, but fortunately only one got injured.

Relieved In Order That They Might Get Sleep. This letter was followed by a postcard with the date February sth on the stamp, acknowledg- ing the receipt of a letter and giving an account of three days' relief from the firing line, during which the deceased rifleman and his comrades were billeted in a school "to enable them to get some sleep at night." He also spoke of con- certs which were given during the relief period, and how the soldiers tried to amuse themselves in various ways. The letter and postcard were received after the sad intelligence of Rifleman Green's death and burial had reached Mrs. Green. In fact, up to the time of writing Mrs. Green had not re- ceived any official communication of her son's death.

Rifleman Edmund Green was the eldest son of the family. His mother's testimony is that he was an exceptionally good son, and the main support at home. When at home he was con- nected with Emmanuel Sunday School Football Club. At the time of his death he had been in the firing line five weeks.

===== 0002601_19150227_art0069.txt | included=true | included =====

In a letter to Mrs. Pearson, written on Febfu- in front doing some odd jobs, and, I think, ven- the Expeditionary Force, Bays :• "It is with great regret that I have to write this-sort of letter to you. lam very sorry to say that Ted Green was shot in the head this morning and died within a couple of minutes.- He was out .ary Bth, Rifleman E. 11. Crook, who is with tured a bit too far and consequently must have been seen by some of the enemy. It happened about 10.30 to 11 o'clock."

===== 0002601_19150227_art0070.txt | included=true | included =====
ONLY TEN DAYS OUT.'

Another %Varringtonian Loses His Life On the Field of Glory.

One of the latest names added to the local Roll of Honour is that of Private J. 13ramhall, No. 12,488, of the 2nd South Lancashire Regiment in France. Mrs. Bramhall, of Lord Nelson-street, off Napier-street, Howley, now a Widow with seven children, whose ages , range from 14 months to 14 years, received a telegram on Shrove Tuesday from the War .offiCe relating to her husband, and concluding in the following terms: "Killed in action at Kennel on 29th January."

===== 0002601_19150227_art0071.txt | included=true | included =====
Fifteen Years in the Volunteers.

Private Bramhall, though a bricklayer by trade, was a soldier at heart, and was 37 years of age when he met his death. Hajoined the Volunteers as bugler when quite a Tdd, and tended 15 successive annual camps. At the out- break of the war he was not long, despite the fact of his being a married man with seven I children of tender age, in making pp his mind that his duty, lay in the service of his country, and he enlisted on September 2nd. Another Fine Marksman.

He was moved about from .place to place, and the discovery was made that Bramhall was a marksman with few equals, and he was quickly hurried to the Front, when only ten days sufficed to end his career. Unlike some soldiers, he was not much of a correspondent, and one brief letter was all Mrs. Bramhall receiVefl from him after his departure from England. This was received on January 24th, five days before his death. The letter merely expresed the hope that the wife and children were all right, and said that he was "all right up to now !" He also expressed his surprise at having seen "Billy " Hughes—also a Warrington man—at the Front:

===== 0002601_19150227_art0072.txt | included=true | included =====
WIRRINGTON TERRITORIALS THE LATE MR. EDWARD HOWARD., ATTHE FRONT. Impressive Funeral of a Prominent Independent Methodist.

Brief announcement ,was made in our last issue of the death of Mr. Edward Howard, of Thornville, ivatchford, who succumbed on Thursday, the 19th inst. His 'passing has been keenly felt by a wide circle of friends and busi- ness intimates, and the funeral on Saturday was the occasion of a marked display of affection and deeply-felt regard. Mr. Howard was born in Eldon-street •43 years ago, and became attached in his youth to the Brick-street Independent Methodist Sunday school. He was able, in maturer years, .to give valuable service to the Sunday school and laboured as a teacher and as its secretary. Hii connection with the Sunday school and with Friars' Green Church continued until a few years ago, when he found a new and fruitful sphere at the church at Grappenhall. His help in every department brought strength to the Church and Sunday school, and made possible the erection of the handsome premises at Latch- ford Without, in which the work is now con- tinued.. Towards the building of the church and school his best endeavour was direeted, and long and earnest effort was given. Many duties fell to him in this undertaking, to all of which he brought fine business instinct and a tact and consideration .which made him a prized and valued colleague. Members of other Independ- ent Methodist Churches widely removed from his own, will feel that the denomination has lost a zealous and devoted member, whose life

===== 0002601_19150227_art0073.txt | included=true | included =====
Ready for the Fray.

OFFICER'S GRAPHIC LETTER

HOME.

An officer in, the Warrington Territorials has written a letter home from France under differ- ent dates, from which we make the following extracts:—

Wears all in the best of spirits and health, and everybody is delighted qt being flung into active service .without further training. Fur coats have been issue(, to officers and men, and we look very quaint in them, a sort of Robinson Crusoe without the limelight. The train is now moving. The men are exceedingly happy and enjoy, the crush of 32 in each cattle truck. The experience is great. Last night we each received a body belt' and pair of socks from the Queen, with the enclosed note in each:

Gift from Mary R. and the women of the Empire; 1914. Buckingham 'Palace. Unfortunately we are not allowed to tell you exactly where we are. It would be much more interesting if you could follow our movements on the map.

At present we are occupying greenhouses, and sleep here to-night, but move again to-morrow to join our Brigade. We arrived here last night after 32 hours in the train, and at once realised how near we were to the fighting line. A terrific artillery can- nonade was taking place, and the whole sky illuminated With the' fldshes of the guns. All this 'taking place within four to six miles away; in faet, our railhead 'Was under the range of the fire. To-day we witnessed two aerial fights, and saw the shrapnel 'bursting from the guns all around: There is a British aero depot within a mile of us, and the aeroplahes have been con- tinuously in the air all day. Immediately a German aircraft is seen up our men rise—and give chase, at the same time anti-aircraft guns opening fire. All last night the big gums continued, but they have be-en comparatively quiet all day, but are rdsuming again to-night. The Germans have been in this town, but do not appear to have done much damage. The whole place is full with our men, mostly old hands at the game.

===== 0002601_19150227_art0074.txt | included=true | included =====
WILL DO CREDIT TO THE TOWN.

Bgfore the week is out we shall have had our baptismal fire. All of us are very cool about what may come, and in the best of spirits. The men are settling down very nicely, and we hope and feel they will do the town and country credit. We have had several nice compliments paid us on the way up, both entraining and detraining. OffiCers' spoke very well of the dis- cipline .and appearance of the men, and they are offiCers of standing and who have seen hun- dreds of thousands go over the railways. We are living entirely on " Tommies' " rations, hard biscuits and bully cheese and jam, but all jolly good when you are hungry.

We were inspected this morning by _Genera

, and told that to-morrow half of the battalion will move to take its place in the trenches. The artillery duel keeps going, ,and reports come in of useful work on our part. A brigade comes in to-night from the trenches for a •week's rest, so.expect to hear some inter- esting news.

To-day has been very wet, and it still pours. You hear the rain pattering on the glass roofs and the rain• water rushing into the tanks. I am writing lying on the grognd by the light of a candle. ,'

I am still in the beet of health and spirits. I have come across Capt. uncle's friend, an officer who was with rot, at Chelsea, and' a Liverpool friend, and as I 'write they are all having breakfast in the same place, the usual biscuits and bully.

Don't be too expectant of letters. Remember, no news is good newts. Don't worry; we are as happy as ever anyone could be, and I would not have missed this experience for anything. If you are sending anything send some strong lades. The heavy boots soon wear the light laces through. Notepaper, envelopes and black leads are particularly useful. We cannot get anything here. Letters often take days to get throxigh.. ,

===== 0002601_19150227_art0075.txt | included=true | included =====
"MAY. BE •IN THE TRENCHES

NOW."

Another officer, writing, in the same strain, adds the following points : None of the men would, like to go back to. Tunbridge Wells for training. Very wet yes- terday, but delightful to-day.

We have been pushed forward and are now within sound of the guns. Last night they were going strong, and the sky was illuminated with shells. A German aeroplane flew close by this morning, but had to hop it. We had good fun watching others being shelled. They man- aged to escape. The Germans have been all over this district and have left their traces, and quite large towns are in total darkness. We came through one last night. It is a weird sight to see the dim outlines ,of houses against the sky. We are all in the pink.

Part of the battalion has been moved up close, and I believe it will go into action first. We are a little bit disappointed, though, of course, someone had to be first. They may be in the trenches by now, and yesterday shells were going over their heads. •

===== 0002601_19150227_art0076.txt | included=true | included =====
TERRITORIALS EQUAL TO REGULARS.

I am glad to say that we shall be treated as Regulars, and are not for lines of communica- tion. , A General of some note told us yesterday that the Territorial Army was doing every bit as well as the Regulars, and that no distinction would be drawn. We were very pleased. We are amongst the " knuts" as regards regiments. We are having a topping time, good grub, light work and reasonable sleep. I have never seen so many aeroplanes as there are here. They were very busy one day, and we heard after- wards that they did good work. As for motors, London is not in it in certain places, especially for commercial vehicles. One of Walker's and another of Greenall's were seen to-day by the men. The mud is dreadful. Captain -- is one of the best. At the present moment he is sitting on a broken box playing a banjo. We are billeted in a place wrapped in mystery, like everything else. For one thing bombs were dropped recently in the vicinity. Milk is brought to us in carts drawn by dogs. They look very picturesque.

I‘AiIitINGTON "TERRIERS" UNDER.

FIRE.

Have 'Already Been In Two Engage-

ments.

Private J:' Podmore, of "A" Company, 4th South Lancashire Regiment—one of Warring- ton's "Terriers "—whose home is at 2, Steel- street, writing to friends in the town, from France, says :

"I am feeling champion and in excellent spirits. We are not far from the firing line. We can hear the guns quite plainly at times. All the boys are in excellent spirits. They still have their little bit of fun. I wish we were having a scrap with the Huns now !" The gallant private's wish has since been gratified, for a subsequent card from him says; We have already been in two engagements.".

THE FUNERAL.

The funeral service, which preceded the inter ment, was held in the Independent Methodisi Church at Latchford. The body was carried by old friends and fellow workers, and was followed by his widow and son, father and mother, and other relatives. Mr. T. Worthington, of Wigan, and Alderman Jas. Evans conducted the ser- vice. In the course of an address Mr. Worth- ington spoke in terms of high appreciation of the labours end charactler of the one whose de- parture had called them together, and expressed their sense of his value to the church and the community, and spoke of his unfailing faith- fulness, diligence and devotion. At the Cemetery the coffin was borne by mem- bers of the office staff of Messrs. R, A. lylor. Alderman Evans read the committal ser- vice at the graveside and briefly addressed the large assembly. The coffin was of polished brown oak and was the work of Messrs. J. Mounfield and Sons, who had charge of the funeral arrangements. The funeral took place on Monday of Mrs. Mary A. Bowers, wife of Mr. J. Bowers, of Thel- wall-view, Grappenhall. The deceased lady succumbed to an attack of pneumonia on Febru- ary 18th. HOSPITALS FOR THE WOUNDED.

Authorities' Proposal to Utilise Asylums. GUARDIANS' OFFER OF NEW

WARDS ACCEPTED

The Warrington Guardians having writteh to the Director General Of the Army Medical Ser- vice offering to place a new block of buildings at the Workhouse at the disposal of his depart- ment for use as a military hospital for sick and wounded soldiers from the Front, that official

has replied to the effect that he mkt gratefully accepts the generous offer, adding that he under- stood that the Guardians were, for the present at any rate, able to accommodate 160 patients or more at an inclusive payment of 2s. per head per day. When offering the use of the bloick the Clerk (Mr. A. Bottomley) had made it clear that the Guardians did not wish to make any profit out of the sick and wounded " Tommies;" but at the same time did not wish to lose any- thing, adding that if the payment offered proved to be inadequate the Guardians would expect any deficiency to be made up, whilst, on the other hand, should it prove that the 2s. per day per man more. than covered the cost, the difference would be refunded. To that the Director General had replied that he thought it would be well if the Board took thi) patients in at 2s. per day, for a few weeks, and that if it was then found that the sum named did not cover the cost the matter of payment could be reconsidered, and he (the Clerk) thought that anything the Guardians might be out of pocket wr

would be reimbursed. The matter was reported to the Warrington Guardians at their fort-nightly meeting on Wednesday, when Mr. Mather said the Board had made their offer in good faith, and he took it that now they would do everything they pos- sibly could to get the building ready for the accommodation of such of the gallant felloics hurt at the Front as might be sent to the War- rington Workhouse.

MILITARY WARDS AT WARRINGTON WORKHOUSE.

The Chairman (Mr. A. J. Jolley) said the Board had already delegated the furnishing of the new block to the Building Committee', and there now arose the question of administration. He took it that they all felt that they did not want to mix up this matter of aiding the wounded soldierg with the ordinary workhouse administration—(hear, hear)—and to that end they some weeks ago appointed a special com- mittee to make the preliminary arrangements. It waruld be necessary, if this special work was to be done properly, to flea' with a number of questions that must arise, as emergency ques- tions—deal with them promptly :and without the formality of a Board meeting. It was understood that the medical administration would be under the supervision of Dr. Hodgkin- son, and the nursing under the supervision of the superintendent nurse, and that the more practical side of the administrative work—the fitting, &o.—would Be under the general control of the Master. But points might arise requir- ing prompt action, and the simplest way to secure that would be to empower the special committee already appointed to deal with such points, and to meet that he proposed that that special committee be empowered to arrange for and control the administration. of the' block

1...AY ••• during its occupation as a Military hospital, keeping separate accounts of all expenditure and paying any additional officers, &c., neces- sary to secure the best possible treatment of patients who might be sent there. The Chairman's proposition, was carried. The special committee to which he referred consists of himself, the Vice-Chairman, the Chairman of the Housing Committee and the Clerk.

The Clerk said that if they had not arranged to have military patients in the new block they would probably have had to use it for the accommodation of lunatics owing to the fact that Winwick Asylum was going to be partly used as a temporary hospital for' siCIIN and wounded soldiers. In thia connection he read a long letter from the Clerk to the Guardians of the township of Manchester, in which it was stated that it was intended at an early meet- ing of that body to make a formal protest against the taking over of one or more of the county asylums for the purpose of providing accommodation for the wounded. The action of the Guardians of the township, wrote their clerk, is not dictated by the slightest spirit of antagonism- to the provision of hospital space for wounded soldiers, because they had already strained the accommodation or their own hos- pitals to make room for such patients; but was due to the fact that for a great number of years extreme difficulty had been experienced in obtaining accommodation for acute cases of mania in the county asylums, which were, and had been for a lengthy period, overcrowded. If such accommodation as there was were re- stricted in the manner proposed, hardship would be inflicted on the 'Unions of the county, and,

in a still greater degree, upon the patients themselves; or, at any rate, those suffering front acute mania.

===== 0002601_19150227_art0082.txt | included=true | included =====
PUBLIC AMUSEMENTS.

ANOTHER PANTO.-AND A GOOD ONE-AT THE ROYAL COURT.

Of the three or four pantomimes which this winter have been staged for the delectation of Warrington playgoers, that which is this week being played at the Royal Court Theatre is, in our opinion, in a good many respects, the best, and it is pretty evident from the enthusiasm of the bumper houses, which are nightly applaud- ing it, that the majority of the patrons of this popular and comfortable house share that opinion with us. The story of "Little Bo- Peep," the nursery heroine, who "lost her sheep and didn't know where to find them," serves as a peg on which to hang as amusing a collection of Vaudeville turns as one could well expect in the compass of two brief hours, whilst oppor- tunity is also afforded for the introduction o? much pretty scenery and many charming dam- sels, who dance pretty dances, sing blithesome choruses, and, generally, add a touch of vivacity to a little show of all-round excellence. Two Fine Plays Next Week. - -

Lovers of the higher -drama are to be catered for next week, when Mr. Ernest E. Norris and his company will present those two brilliant London successes, "The Walls of Jericho," by Alfred Sutre—one of the greatest of our play- wrights—and "The Lion and the Mouse." The last-named will be given on Friday night only. Both plays are amongst the finest modern con- tributions to dramatic art.

===== 0002601_19150227_art0083.txt | included=true | included =====
SONG AND SMARTNESS AT ME

HIPPODROME.

Premier place in a fine bill at this popular place of entertainment is given to " Violet and , Pink," a musical revue which, having scored ; a big hit at the London Pavilion, is now coming into the provinces to repeat its Metropolitan success. It is a smart and distinctly amusing, not to mention pretty, production and local lovers of vaudeville are "going nap" on it. It is the very essence of frolicsomeness, and the music, which forms one of its strong features, is tunefully fascinating, whilst 'original, scenery is decidedly novel and refreshingly original, par- ticularly that scene in which a party of tourists view London from a balloon. The merry trifle is merrily played by a company sent "on the road" by Messrs. Braham and Campbell, and, if you like a good laugh and an ear-catching jingle, you may take our word for it that "Violet and Pink" is well worth seeing. There's oceans of fun in it, a host of pretty girls, and the music, like the mirth, is above the "cheap and nasty" order. Several other turns go to the making of an excellent entertainment.

Dramatic Morality Play Next Week. The big attraction for next week will be Alfred Wood's company in "Saint and Sinner," a pow- erful morality play which has achieved some- think akin to a sensation wherever it has been presented. Another strong lino in the bill will be provided by Mr. Charles Hanbury and Co. in "His Last Night Out," a farcical conceit which is calculated to leave any audience prostrate with over-much laughter.

THE , BOY OF TO-DAY:

How Does He Compare with His Grandfather?

WHY MR. SINGDON RESIGNED.

At the annual meeting of the Warrington Chambei of Commerce, held in the Town Ilan on Monday, Mr. Holman Kingdon presented a report detailing the activities of the Education Committee—on which he sits as the Chamber's representative—during the past year. In the course of his report, Mr. Kingdon said that during 1914 the policy of the local Educa- tion Authority had been steadily pursued in the direction indicated in previous years, and pro- gress could again be reported.

On former occasions attention had been drawn to the insufficient accommodation in the schools and the pressing need for the erection of fur- ther buildings. Warrington' had had to spend a good deal of money during the last few years, which ought to have been divided over a longer period; in other words, the present committee had had to make up for .the shortcomings of 'their predecessors; and the fact that at the beginning of last year the Board of Education Actually reduced the grants to certain schools because the accommodation was insufficient, ,showed how very necessary it Was for' that .inoncty to be spent.

===== 0002601_19150227_art0086.txt | included=true | included =====
The Shortcomings of the Modern Boy.

In considering the cost of education to the town, which again shows an increase in the rate per X, due consideration must be given both to the shortcomings of the past and also the needs for the future; but the method of criticism both in this and in other similar matters should be not: What is the rate per X? but rather: What are we getting for our money? It was often said that there was no adequate return for the enormous sums now being spent in this country on elementary education. Boys when they leave school did not seem to know any more than they used to do, and he (Mr. Kingdoii) must confess that he was often dis- appointed at the lack of appreciation of the enormous advantages offered to the boy of to- day compared with those possessed by his fore- fathers; while certainly in good manners, habits of discipline, and behaviour to those older, there is still much to be desired.

A Nation Like a Cricket Team. Perhaps the war in these matters would not be an unmixed evil. At present they were in a transition stage—the full results of their educa- tional investment had yet to be felt, but those who had gone through. this time of national stress and peril would, at the end, have a saner national thought and -Would 'appreciate that, after all, a nation had to be run very much like a cricket team, each doing his own work in his 0 proper place and all for the benefit of the team. Perhaps the most important feature of the educational Work during 1914 was the setting up of two University scholarships, and it was now possible for any boy or girl to proceed from an elementary school, through the Secondary School, to the University. Thus in Warrington had the educational ladder been completed, and • it would be the nation in the long run that would reap the advantage. ___ The/Who-Shall-Appoint? Controversy;

He could not conclude his report Without making special reference to the work of the School Medical Inspection Committee, of which for nearly five years he had had the honour of being chairman. What he wished to particu- larly allude to was the appointment of a whole-

time dentist, which marked a new era in the work of the dental clinic. Up to the present a part-time dentist had been engaged, but now the work had increased to an extent which, it was felt, 'would justify the appointment of a whole-

time man. He regretted to say that although the p_art- time dentist was appointed by this committee, the Health Committee had claimed the right of appointing the whole-time dentist, and they thus had the impossible position of the com- mittee who was responsible for the work dorie not having any voice or influence in the ap- pointments of the officials who had to do it.

Why He Resigned! About a year ago the matter was fully die- cussed with the Health Committee, and it was thought that in the interests of the town and The work on behalf of the children—for after all it waf those who would suffer by any inter- ruption ih theJpolicY or progress of the remedial -"- work that had been undertaken by this cora- mittee—some 'arrangement would be made which _ would enable the two committees to work to- gether under the Medical Officer of Health, who was also the principal School Medical Officer. But nothing was done, and he had, therefore, with the very greatest reluctance, felt that no other course was open to him but , to retire from the chairmanship and leave the position open to someone who would be more in sym- pathy with the existing regulations. He thought the Health Committee had not taken a broad view of the situation. The local Bduch- tion Authority had certain duties imposed upon them by Statute, and -those duties were dek- gated to ,the Medical Inspection Committee. The latter must therefore always be a sub-com- mittee of the .Education Committee, and not, as some would appear to think, of the Health Com- mittee. The proper and only way for the Health Committee to influence those appoint- ments, if they wished to do so, was by becom- ing members of the Medical Inspection Com- mittee,- as some of them had done already, and taking their share in the work as well as the patronage. The report was received.

Death From Burns'.

Beatrice Parkin, eleven years of age, daughter of Charles Parkin, of Hotel-street, Earlestown, was in the act of putting a kettle of water on the fire at her home on Wednesday when her pinafore caught fire. She ran out into the street and thus ignited the rest of her clothing, with the result that she was badly burned abdut the upper part of the body. Dr. Elliot, of Earles- town, ordered her removal to Warrington-In- firmary, where she arrived at 7.15 the same night and died from shock four hours later.

===== 0002601_19150227_art0095.txt | included=true | included =====
Life On Ten Shillings A Year.

"Native Life' in the Plains of Bengal" ',Was the title of a most interesting illustrated lecture given to the members of the Warrington Photo- graphic Society on Tuesday night by Mr. Had- field, of Du.kinfield. The lecturer described life in Cakutta and other large Bengali towns, and among other interesting details mentioned thht the natives who were employed in watering the streets are only paid 2s. 6d. a month, and, though they only work from February to May, manage to save enough out of that preposter- ously small wage to keep themselves the re- mainder of the year.

Warringtonian's Death In Canada.

Many Wa-rringtonians will regret to hear of the death at his home, High Park, Niagara on the Lake, Canada, of Mr. Edwin Sharpies, a joiner, who formerly worked for Bylands' Bros. (where his father is still employed), and Messrs. Monks, Hall and Co. Mr. Sharpies, who was only 33 years of age, went to Canada eighteen months ago, and four months- later sent for his wife and family. He died on January 28th, leaving three young children, but the local newspaper says he had earned the friendship and esteem of all with whom be had come into eoeta,ct and that universal sympathy was felt tor' his widQw.

===== 0002601_19150227_art0098.txt | included=true | included =====
The Religion of the Ancient Greeks.

At the Academy-street Adult School on. Sun- 'day evening, Professor G. Currie Martin, of London, delivered the first of a series of four lectures on "The Lessons of the Great Reli- gions." The first of his lectures dealt with the religion of JOcient Greec. e. and was of a most interesting description. The religion of the by- gone Greek, he told a large audience, was rather of ,an adaptable kind, and it succeeded in wak- ing men feel at home in this life; in fact, it anade them rather too 'much at home in the world. That was, perhaps, Why they produced •sueli marvellous poetry devoted to showing how beautiful the world was in the splendour and wonder of its sunsets and of the other things that appertained more to the grosser existence than to the idyllic and the eternal—poetry the like of which no race had ever reproduced. The second of the series of lectures which has been arranged by the Warrington Adult School Sub- -Union Education Coromittee will be given to- morrow night, Professor Currie Martin's subject befog "The Religion of Hinduism—Fate." All .are invited. ,

===== 0002601_19150227_art0099.txt | included=true | included =====
Wanted: More Thinking ; Less Drinking.

"Be Fit!" was the title of the address given ty' Mr. E. Lloyil Jones at the usual Pleasant Afternoon at WYcHffe Church on. Sunday. He .tniphasised the Christian's duty to keep himself lie: They looked, he said, for fitness in the Pulpit and expected it, too; but it was just 'as needful in the pew. It was an obligation that devolved upon every Christian to look after the body, as a healthy body went to,make a healthy mind. Neglect of the body brought physical tuin, and,* very often, spiritual ruin as well. One could see the effects of neglect of the body

in the streets every day of life. Think was one the most frequent causes of injury to the hody; whilst it also played havoc with the 14Tiiritual side of man. The body was a machine that must be well looked after, or it soon came to 'a stop. Besides physical fitness, there was mental fitness, and that also was of the greatest importance. A great need of the times was more thinking and less drinking; indeed, thinking men were never more needed than they were in these days of crises. True fitness consisted in a healthy mind in a healthy body. To attain a healthy mind they must keep their thoughts clean—they must read right and give their minds only healthy matter to digest.—The Rev. T. T. James, who presided, again urged the members, to do all in their power to make the P.S.A. Brotherhood's twenty-second anniversary on'. March :7th, a complete success. During the afternoon Mrs. Smallshaw sang "Abide With Me.," whilst Mr. Payton presided at the organ.

The new Ambulance Class to which Dr. Manson is kindly giving the lectures of the first-aid course stipulated by the St. John Am- bulance Association, began at the Police Build- ings last night. WILTON SHOOTING AFFAIR.

'Elder Brother Found Guilty and the Younger Discharged.

Before Mr. Justice Lush, at the Chester Assizes, on Thursday, Frank Hughes, jun., (18), greengrocer, of Brook Cottages, Lower Walton, Warrington, was charged with shooting at Wallace Edgar Smith, an assistant gamekeeper, with intent to murder him, and his brother, Thomas Hughes (16), blacksmith's apprentice, was charged with procuring and commanding Frank Hughes to commit the offence.

Both the boys pleaded not guilty. Mr. Artemus Jones (instructed by Messrs. Hy. Greenall and Co.) prosecuted, and Mr. Trevor Lloyd (instruclted by Mr. T. S. Steel) defended. Mr. Jones stated that Smith was an assistant gamekeeper to Sir Gilbert Greenall, Walton Hall. On November 22 (Sunday) Smith was near Walton Old Hall Wood. It would be about seven o'clock in the morning, and fairly light. Whilst on the watch he saw these two lads, who passed him eight or nine yards away. As they repassed him Smith stepped out. Frank Hughes had the gun in his possession. Smith knocked the gun up, and Thomas Hughes called upon his biother to shoot, and come on. Frank raised the gun, deliberately took aim at him, and fired. Smith put up his left hand to save himself, and received shots in the hand and face. Later he was treated at Dr. Howard's surgery, where he was found to be suffering from twenty freshly-inflicted bleeding wounds on the left side of the upper part of the chest, one in the root of the neat, and several about the left hand and arm. The injured youth was now perfectly well again. The witnesses for the prosecution having been heard,

Frank Hughes, the elder prisoner, gave evi- dence on his own behalf, and said he shot Smith accidentally, having intended to shoot past him to frighten him. Thomas Hughes said that when he said to his brother "Shoot !" he meant him to shoot to frighten, but not to hit Smith.

Mr. Trevor Lloyd, for the defence, submitted that. the wounding was accidental, and that there was no intent about the act. Frank was guilty of unlawful wounding only. Thomas was out of the plantation when the shooting took place, and could not be held responsible for what his brother did.

The jury found the prisoner Prank Hughes guilty of unlawful wounding, and Thomas not guilty. The latter was discharged. With regard to Frank, the Judge said he would like to deal with him under the Borstal system, but he understood there was some reason why he could not do so, because he was a cripple, and could not do the Swedish drill. He therefore postponed sentence. On Friday the prisoner was ordered twelve months' detentibn under a modified Borstal system.

THE REV. AAr. BRACECAMP. A Keen, Energetic and Sympathetic

Worker.

To the Editor of "The Examiner."

Sir,—Readers of "The Examiner" will be much interested to learn that the Rev. IW. Bracecamp, chaplain third class—which ranks as Major in the Army—is doing good work with the troops in Tunbridge Wells. It was a great disappointment to the 4th Sattalion South Laneashires that their genial and popular "Padre" was not allowed to go abroad. He had won the hearts of the officers and men.

When the regiment went out on field days, marching cheerfully with the men was always to be seen the "Padre." On Sundays he has a very busy day from early morn till late at night. Chaplain liracecamp is a keen and energetic worker, and a sympathetic friend to all the men garrisoned in and around Tunbridge Wells. If ever he was called away from Tunbridge Wells he would be greatly missed by all the soldiers and .the various churches and hospitals where •he labours.

I hope the good people of his church in War- rington Will not, recall him while he is doing such igood work here. I an sending you this letter in order that it may reach the members of his own congrega- tion.

Yours faithfully,

EXPERT° CREDE.

===== 0002601_19150227_art0105.txt | included=true | included =====
SAFEGUARDS AGAINST THE AIR- PERIL.

'Warrington To Be Plunged Into Semi-

Gloom.

Colonel J. A. Moggridge, who confinands the 40th Regimental District, which embraces War- rington, has issued an important order with regard to the regulation of lighting in the town, but specially points out that his order is only a measure of precaution against possible attacks by an enemy, and need cause no alarm to the "public. The order demands, among other things, that in all brightly-lighted streets and squares, and ~particularly on bridges and ele- vated positions, a portion of the lights must be extinguished so as to break up all conspicuous groups and rows of lights,- and that the remain- ing lights must be lowered or made invisible from above. The intensity of the inside light- ing of all buildings must be reduced, powerful headlights on motor cars are prohibited, all large lighted roof areas'must be covered over, and the lights of. tramcars and omnibuses and railway stations must only be just sufficient to enable business to be carried on " as usual" with reasonable safety. In case of sudden emergency all instructions given by the military authorities or the Chief Constable as to the fur- ther reduction; or extinction, of lights must be immediately obeyed. Any contravention of the terms of the order—which is set out in full in our advertisement columns—is punishable by a fine not exceeding £lOO, or by imprisonment. The order comes into force on March 4th..

THE EXAMINER, SATURDAY, FEBRUARY '27, 1915.

MAKING GOOD SOLDIERS.

How Comrades-in-arms Are Preparing to Meet the Huns.

PLEASANT BILLET LIFE.

BY FRANK KAY.

Every day now I am becoming moli3 -,of a soldier, and my former self is fast fading away into the dim distance. The transformation has to be brought about with rapidity, because the peed for trailed men is so great and pressing at the present time, but, although men have to be drilled as quickly as possible, they are not being turned out inefficient or lacking in, the quali- fications which go to make a good soldier.. -

Our " Domestic " Comfort. I have said before that the food is good, although roughly served, and I am still of that opinion. I think it might be of interest if I were to explain what we actually get in the , way of "grub." Here are the details Breakfast: Tea, bread and butter, tomatoes and bacon, fish or meat, and sometimes jam. Dinner: Stewed meat or steak pie, peas, beans or cabbage, potatoes, and pudding, sometimes with jam. • Tea: Fish, cold Meat, sardines or cheese, bread and butter and jam, and tea. For supper, I understand, they have stew or bread and cheese, but we, being away from the barracks:and billeted at the schools, do not get any supper—unless we purchase it ourselves— because we could not get back from barracks in time for bed at 9.15. As to the manner in which the food is served, I might say' that all the men are given a meal check and file into a waggon shed one behind the other in turn. Each man has a knife, fork and spoon, which he has to keep to himself, and as he files into the shed his check is taken from him by one of the checkers on duty, and then be goes round to the " Tommies " who serve the food. out. The meat is first put on a tin plate, and then the .men go forward to the vegetable servers, and then to those who "dish out" the pudding, which is put on a thick chunk of bread. Then the diners take their places at the long row of hare, but clean, tables, and "tuck in." The meal finished, each man takes the things he has been using, empties the leav- ings—if there are any, of course—and then re- ceives another meal ticket as he is going out. It is laughable to see the men pushing .their .knives and forks in the ground after a meal in order to clean them. "A primitive way," you say? Anyhow, it is a very effective way, and the articles are always clean.

As to the quantity of food, there is plenty for every man, and he* is considered to have an ex- ceptionally good appetite Who "clears" the lot. The quality of the food is all right, too, for almost every day a lieutenant visits the shed whilst a meal is in progress and asks, "Are there any Complaints?" and from the thousands of men who are here I have not heard a single one. There is a saying that "If you sleep well and eat well you will not take much harm," so the Germans need have no fear that our men will not be " all there," because from what I have seen, the men sleep "like tops" and eat like young horses. \ For a bed, we have a straw mattress and pillow, with three thick and warm "horse" blankets. The blankets have -to be folded up neatly every morning, and placed, along with the kit bag, on the top of the turned up mat- tress. The art of making the bed ready for the night is very soon acquired, and one soon gets to know which way to make up the blankets so that one will be warm and comfortable during the night. Each plan has a "comforter," which acts as a scarf in the day time and which, at night, can be transformed into a warm sleeping cap. It is a very funny sight to go into.a.`"bed- room"—ours is at present a classroom—and see all the sleeping caps poking out of the beds. The Day's Work.

We have had a very_strenuous week of drill- ing. In the early hours of the morning the corporal comes round and shouts, "Now, then, show a leg there and fall in," and the men soon tumble out and get ready. Each rbom is in charge of an orderly, whose duty it is to keep everything nice and tidy and look after things generally. We have a very nice and obliging orderly in our room, who is always willing to, do anyone a favour and look after any little private things that are left about by the men in order to be handy for use. As I write one " Tommy " has just been assured by the orderly that his socks are quite dry through being in front of the fire for a day. We start drilling about 8.30 a.m. and go on until 12 noon. We start again at 2 o'clock and finish at four. If we are not detailed for any "fatigue" work at night we are free to do as we like after tea. The wet nights are spent very pleasantly at one of the homes, or in cracking jokes or retailing news around the fire in the room where we sleep. My chief concern this week Mrs been to try and get in with one of the parties detailed for draft, but up to the present I have been un- guccessful. Parties for transfer are being made up rapidly, and olio never knows when one's own turn will come. The other 'night we were awakened about 12 o'clock and told to answer our names for drafts. My surname was called out, and with joy I answered "Hire, Sergeant," only to be disappointed, when I told my Chris- tian name, to be informed that it was not me they wanted. Never mind, perhaps my chance will come in a day or two. If sent on a draft I shall be 'drilled thoroughly ready . " FOY France" and the Huns. "The Fellows."

And now about, the fellows who are here.' They are all a jolly crew and more like a lot of grown-up schoolboys—in ways of friendship, not in strength and endurance—than' what one imagines soldiers to be like. One of the com- pany may be sick. He is cheered up. by his companions and recommended to try• several "sure cures." At present one of the men has just received a parcel from home containing cakes. As soon as opened he hands the cakes round, and we all munch and congratulate the unknown confectioner on the result of his or her handiwork. Another "Tommy" is boiling water with which to make some "Oxo" from the handy little _cubes, and if he repeats his _ _ _ _ _

action onast night hoz:will give the first drink of the invigorating beverage to one of the chaps who has been confined to bed for a day or two. This spirit of good fellowship is in existence all over the barracks, and I will leave it to the reader's imagination—and he need not stretch it much—to form an idea as to what it leads to ultimately. Of course, one has to stand a good deal of good-humoured banter, such as "Does she send you many kisses?" when you get a letter, or "Have you not paid that account yet?" You've got to take it all in good part,

for it is not said to cause any offence. You're turn comes, and, of course, you enjoy a joke at someone else's expense. Anyhow, no one laughs at you when you are occupied in sewing buttons on your clothes, or doing any other rich work, because every man knows he has to do it, and that if jokes are made about it they will/ get stale and will not "fire."

Well, I shall have to make my bed now, and turn in, for it is 8.45. Early, isn't it? But remember, "Early to bed, &c.," and also the fact that by 5.30 in the morning we shall be marching down to barracks, singing "Tip- perary," and other popular songs, and shall be ready for another day's work.

HOME FROM THE FRONT.

Private A. Povey is at present at his home, West View-terrace, ,Longford, Warrington, suf.- fering from rheumatism. An Army Reservist' he re-joined his regiment, the Cheshires, at the outbreak of war, and going to the Front, was wounded at La Basses, on the afternoon of Christmas Day, being shot •clean through the arm. Whilst recovering from the wound he developed ,rheumatism, and in consequence his sick leave has been eztended.

LORD DERBY'S APPEAL FOR ARTILLERYMEN.

The Mayor and Mr. A. H. Crosfield Support His Lordship.

Warrington, which is so closely connected With - the South Lancashire Regiment, has as yet no artillery unit, and this fact was described last ,week by Lieut.-Colonel Lyon, of Appleton, as "I a very serious blot." But it is a blot that is going to be speedily removed, and already' such a unit is in course of formation, largely as the result of a great recruiting meeting which was held in the Parr Hall on Wednesday even- ing, and was.addressed by Lord Derby, who has been charged with, and is most energetically discharging the day, of raising -Divisional Royal Field Artillery, on the principle of the Comrades Battalions, in Lancashire, the idea being that Warrington shall furnish the men for at least one battery in the local brigade. The local recruiting officers will enlist men desirous of serving, and they will be engaged under exactly the same conditions as to pay and allowances as the Regular Royal Artillery- men; whilst they will be trained.at Lytham and St. Anne's, where everything possible will be done for their comfort.

Wednesday night's meeting was not, perhaps, quite so large as.others of a like kind that have been held in the same spacious building, but it made up for a slight lacking in numbers by the intensity of its enthusiasm, and there can be little doubt that the object for which it was called will be achieved.

in Lancashire, save,- perhaps, St. Helens and Liverpool.

A NEW NOTE OF WARNING.

He was there again_that night to give them a note of warning, and he hoped it would not happen that when he came to the town again he would have to say "I told you so, although you didn't agree with me!". He told them that night, as distinctly and as forcibly as he eould, that unless they got more men, and still more men, this country and her Allies were not going to prove quickly successful in the war. Every day the war lasted was involving the country in a huge expenditure of money and lives; an ex- penditure which would hinder the future devel- opment of the nation. On the other hand every day which they could shorten the war by was so much •gained for the prosperity of those for whom they ought to hayse consideration—those who were going to come after. In making his appeal that, night they might think it extra- ordinary if he said that he did not want to see railway men or men engdged on Government contracts come forward. Po such men he said: " If you will put in a real hard day's work, and make good the shortage of men that undoubt- edly exists, owing to so many of your comrades having joined the colours, you Will be doing just as good a service to your country as the men at the Front, though yours may not be the same glory." (Hear, hear) • Whilst he would be. glad to see the marrsied men, come forward, the man he specially appealed. to' was the young unmarried man, and he could assure such young men that if they came forward, those dependent upon them would, under a new Government Order, receive ample benefit; (Applause.)

the cheery geniality which in combination, with his well-known energy, sagacity and experience, distinguished Lord Derby, and had won for him such well-deserved and such universal popular- ity. (Applause.) Nothing could have been more direct or telling than the appeal which his Lordship had made that night, and, as the result of it, he (the speaker) hoped to see the young fellows of Warrington tumbling over each other's heels in their hurry to respond. (Hear, hear.)

THE IMPORTANT WORK OF THE STAY-

AT-HOMES.

He was struck by, and was in hearty accord with, What Lord Derby had said about the im-, portance of the work now being done by scores of thousands of men on the railways, and in the factories and workshops of the country. Much as the nation as a whole respected and valued that work—which was indeed indispensable for the final triumph of the Allies—the men engaged upon it would be the first to recognise, as indeed they all recognised, that their chief 'con- gratulations in' these days, as well as the highest

honours, were reserved, and rightly reserved, for those who had the courage to go to the Front, and who would there have the privilege of playing a never-to-be-forgotten part with our forces and the Allies of our country, and so finally helping to, turn the scale in the present great struggle. (Hear, hear, and cheers.) They had not yet arrived at the moment when,the scale had been finally turned, and from all accounts there were yet, in front of the Allies a few months of strenuous work before they could make Germany change her key. In London,. only last month, he was talking to.one of the captains of British indus- try, who owned several large business concerns in Germany, and who, having received permis- sion from the Bnglish War Office„ had just been across to Holland to interview the chiefs of his large factories in Germany.

GERMANY "SICK TO DEATH OF THE

WAR."

He (Mr. Crosfield) asked his friend what the opinion of the German people on the war was— what they had to 'say about it, so far as he could gather from the directors of his factories. "Oh !" his friend replied, "all they said made it clear to me that one and all of them are sick to death of the war already. But when it came to discussing terms on which peace may be arranged, I found them in a state of mind that is absolutely impossible and preposterous. All they talked about, all they thought about, was the amount of com- ..

pensation that could be made, and should be made to Germany for her sacrifices in the war." (Loud laughter.) That rather suggested to him (the speaker) the case of the burglar who, having made a raid upon a house, demanded compensation from the police who arrested .him. (Laughter.)

MUCH DONE, BUT MORE REMAINS.

The May Appeal to the Young Men

in the Streets.

• His WORSHIP, in opening the meeting, said that the splendid manner in which Lancashire had already answered the call to arms made everyone who belonged to the county proud of it. Warrington had not lagged behind; War- rington nevec did. (Applause.) The South Lancashire Regiment, strengthened by hundreds of Territorials, was already in the firing line, and anxious though those left at home might feel, they were nevertheless also proud that their lads should have come forward and offered themselves in upholding in the face of relent- less foes the principles of good faith, honour and justice, which to. every Englishman were dearer, even than life itself. (Applause.) But more remained to be done, and the splendid efforts that had already been put forth should only spur them on to even greater things. And here he would like to say most emphatically that, splendidly as those men who had come for- ward had done, the young men of Warrington as a whole had not done their duty. PAARRIEDIAEN HAVE DONE GLORIOUSLY By that he meant the young men who had not got dependents. But the married men ot War- rington had done gloriously. (Applause.). He would like, if possible, to compel every single man, who had not got some serious responsibility, to join the Army. (Hear, hear.) They ought to under- stand their duty in this matter. •When he walked down the streets at nights and saw the young men promenading them, it made his blood boil as he thought that, instead of more or less aimlessly wandering up and down, they ought to be in the firing line, helping their com- rades. (Hear, hear.) In conclusion, his Wor- ship trusted that Lord Derby's recruiting visit might prove successful. (Applause...) .
