YOUNG MEN-COME AND PLAY YOUR

PART!

There must be many more suitable young men in Warrington, and he appealed to them to come forward and play their part, so that the succeeding generation would not be able to say to them, "You didn't play your part in the great war! You did not help England! You did nothing to help to retain her prosperity and her freedom! Why should we consider you?" He believed that, even now, the young men of the country did not quite understand the whole position; did not quite realise that this country had been fighting now for six months, that they had suffered over a hundred thousand casual- ties, whilst the Germans had lost over two mil- lion men, and that yet, save in a very small corner, the Germans were able to fight all their battles on somebody else's soil; and that the Allies were not going to have a really great success until they had pushed their foes right back into their own territory and were in a position to dictate peace on their . own terms—terms which would ensure peace not only for themselves, but for their children and their children's children. (Hear, hear.) Having dis- claimed any idea of being a conscriptionist, his Lordship went on to say: "But if we had hfid National Service along the lines that I have helped to make known throughout the country, every man who has, now come forward would have come forward just the same, but with this important difference, that he would have come forward with instruction, instead of having to come forward untrained and wait until he had been trained before he .could render that ser- vice for which he had offered' himself. (Hear, hear.)

TO SMASH GERMAN MILITARISM.

But it was no use crying over spilt milk, and

they had now to do the best they could to smash up German militarism finally—for ever! (Hear, hear.) No half-hearted peace would do; they had got to see to it that the peace was arranged on such tbrms that for many and many a,lifetime Germany had got to be reduced to the position of a negligible quantity in European affairs. When that had been done, then a great part of that m?ney which this country had now to spend on her Army and Navy might be de- voted to social reform. Prussian militarism— for, after all, it was Prussian rather than Ger- man—had been the curse of the lifetime of all gathered in that hall, and unless they utterly smashed it, now that the chance was theirs, it would be the curse of those who came after.

(Hear, hear.) His Lordship went on to point out that besides assisting him in raising this Artillery Brigade, Warringtonians had another duty, and that was to do their part in keeping the South Lancashire Regiment—which had had many a hard knock, and given one—(applause) —up to its strength. Again, the other day, they sent abroad one of the finest Territorial Bat- talions that had ever left these shores—(cheers) —and it was up to the young men of the town to keep that also at full strength. (Hear, hear.) The best tribute they could pay to those gallant lads was not by clapping their hands at men- tion of them, but by seeing to it that as their 'numbers were 'thinned by death, by wound, or by disease, other Warringtonians were ready to take the vacant places in their ranks. (Hear, hear.)

===== 0002601_19150227_art0117.txt | included=true | included =====
THE MAGNIFICENT WORK OF THE ARTILLERY.

No branch of the service had done more mag- nificent work in the war than had the artillery, and he felt that they were not giving the infan- try they had raised in the County Palatine fair play, unless they also gave them the artillery, which was necessary to prepare the way for

;hem to advance along. (Hear, hear.) There- !ore he begged all who were able to come_ for-

ward and -assist in raising this new artillery force. Lytham and St. Armes were at the dis-

posal of such as came forward. The inhabitants

were on their knees before the recruits, prepared to feed them and look after them in every way they could. (Laughter and applause.) They would be hard worked; he could promise them that, but he could also promise them that they would be well looked after, and that they would not have to wait for their uniforms. (Ap- plause.) Those responsible for raising the new regiment would do all in their power for the men, who, in their turn, were prepared to do everything they possibly could for their coun- try. (Cheers.) "The honour of England is at stake," said his Lordship, in conclusion, "and I believe that honour is dear to 'every young man in this room, and therefore every one of them ought to Some forward and play his part in defending that honour." (Cheers.)

===== 0002601_19150227_art0118.txt | included=true | included =====
MR. CROSFIELD AND THE PRIVI- LEGE OF SERVING.

Germany Must Be Taught A Deterrer

Lesson.

Mr. Alum?. H. CROSFIELD, who was accorded a flattering reception, then proposed a hearty vote of thanks to the speakers. Having re- ferred to the pithy and pointed addresses by General Abdy and Colonel Lyon, Mr. Crosfield proceeded to make a graceful reference to the straight appeal to the young men of Warrington which had been made by the Mayor, and went on to &fly that they were all full of admiration and respect for the inde- fatigable zeal which Lord Derby had thrown himself into the patriotic task which had brought him to that meeting—a task which was of the highest value to the country at the present time. (Applause.) Lord Derby- had been absolutely untiring in the efforts that he had made; efforts which had increased his in- fluence among the people of Lancashire—an influence that was already great in the old days in the House of Commons, and was now even greater "in another place.". '(Laughter and 4 1,014 A Laa Alai Uellee IV giAlta.llCed

===== 0002601_19150227_art0119.txt | included=true | included =====
BRITAIN'S BRAVE PART:

The people of the Fatherland had got to change their key; their whole frame of mind had got to be changed, and it rested with the people of this country to help them to do that; nay,. more, to make up their minds for them. (Laughter and applause.) Russia, in all conscienee, had enough on her hands at the present time and they could admire the doggedness of the soldiers of that great Empire, just as they admired the splendid work which their French Allies had accomplished, and the heroic conduct of the Belgians and the Servians. (Applause.) When they came to consider the economic resources of Britain, her manufactur- ing capacity—the power, which would tell more and more as the struggle went on, to provide equipnient—the indomitable zeal of our gallant blue jackets,and the enthusiasm,the fire and the courage with which Britain's dauntless soldiers were fighting, he said, not boastfully, but sin- cerely, that he felt that Britain's effort and energy were, in the long 'run, going to be the chief factor in the war. (Applause.) And when the fight had been won, the triumph would be so great that he sometimes wondered whether we would not have to offer up prayer three times a day that we did not get an attack of swelled head, like that which had been both- ering the Kaiser lately. After all, he did not feel any great anxiety on that point, because really the catastrophe in store for the Father-' land—and "the ramshackle Empire "—was so terrible and would be so awful a tragedy that it would give all men pause. (Hear, hear.) In all sincerity, he thought, whatever the hard- ships, the risks, and the sacrifices the soldiers of this country had to face, those who perforce had to remain at home could indeed congratu- late them on the privilege which was theirs— and he put it no lower than that; for it was a r4al privilege—to take part in and help to carry through BO great an achievement. Not once in a thousand years did it fall to the men of our race to address themselves, and devote their energies and lives to accomplishing so great a task. It would be impossible to rate too highly, or to exaggerate the magnitude of, the work the Allied Forces had in hand. Let them ask themselves what success in this war meant for Old England. (Hear, hear.) What it meant for the whole of the British Empire; nay, more, for the entire civilised world. (Cheers.) Let them also remember they had to administer a lesson which would be a deter- rent in all future history whenever again any nation was tempted by reckless and criminal ambition to run amok against the whole civil- ised world.

===== 0002601_19150227_art0120.txt | included=true | included =====
INTERNATIONAL MISCREANT'S IMPEACHMENT.

They had to administer that lesson, and they had also to give severe and terrible punishment' to the international miscreant who had brought about the present European catastrophe. He did not think they could better express • their indictment of the enemy, their overwhelming charge against him, than in the words used long ago by a great Englishman on a very historic occasion; words which could be used to-day with much better, and more unqualified justi- fication, than the orator had when he spoke in Westminster Hall. He gave them his lan- guage with but the alteration of a syllable or two:

"Therefore hath it with all confidence been ordered by the Commons of Great Britain that we impeach our Prussian enemy of high crimes and misdemeanours. We impeach him in the name of the English nation, whose ancient honour he would fain have forced us to sully; we impeach him in the name of the people of Belgium, whose rights he has trodden under- foot, and whose country he has turned into a desert; lastly, in the name of human nature itself, in the name of both sexes, in the name of every age, in the name of every rank, we impeach the common enemy and oppressor of all." (Applause.)

===== 0002601_19150227_art0121.txt | included=true | included =====
A PAGE FROM HISTORY.

The Derby Family and Warrington.

Alderman BENNETT seconded, remarking that he was quite sure that the result of that meet- ing would be seen in a. considerable number of new recruits. (Hear, hear.) The Alderman then went on to refer in inter- esting fashion to visits paid to the town by ancestors of Loid Derby, and to their connec- tion with Warrington, recalling, for instance, the fact that it was a Derby who built War- rington's third bridge in 1495, and how, in com- pany with the Lard Derby of that time King

Henry the Seventh rode* o'ver the newly-built bridge, thus creating one of the most famous passages in local history. They would recall the fact that the present Lord Derby was asso- ciated with the present • arid sixth bridge, for did he not accompany King George the Fifth, when, a few months ago, his Majesty- came to formally open it? In 1643 another Lord Derby was closely connected with Warrington's his- tory, for he defended the town on behalf of King Charles against the Cromwellian forces, and subsequently laid down, his life for his King.

ROYALIST LORD WHO FIRED THE TOWN.

It was recorded of this Lord Derby that, when he held Warrington, "Sir William Brereton, accompanied by Col. Roswarm, the valiant and faithful German engineer, begirt the town about and fiercely assaulted • it.' ' They had gotten Sankey Bridge . . . and some of the outer walls, and within a short space of time were likely to have the whole, whereupon the Lord Derby set the middle of the town on fire, pro- testing he would rather burn, it all ere the enemy should have it." (Cheers.)' Turning to the subject of the meeting, the Alderman said he' did not know Whether the young men of to-day fully realised the tremen- dous gravity of the present crisis/ he was rather afraid some of them did not !-:-Land the fact that it was necessary that more and yet more of them should offer themselves if the present struggle was to be brought to an early and suc- cessful issue. The speaker also recalled the .Words which Colonel O'Leary—whose statue in their midst was a possession much prized by Warringtonians—addressed to his men shortly before he laid down his life for his country at the battle of Pieters Hill: "Remember, men, the eyes of Lancashire are upon you!" It was 'a noble battle-cry, and it was equally tine of to-day, and he: would like every young War- ringtonian to take it to heart. (Applause.)

===== 0002601_19150227_art0122.txt | included=true | included =====
WOUNDED ARTILLERYMAN'S APPEAL.

The proposition was put and carried with loud applause, but before Lord Derby replied, Driver PETEIER, of the 87th Battery of the Royal Field Axtillery—one of the wounded patients at Red- den Court Red Cross Hospital—said a few words, which were cheered to the echo. "Artillery," he said, "is the one thing we do want. It has

been badly needed from the start. We have had to fight hard battles because we have had such big numbers against us, but as soon as we get as many men out there as the Germans Wave got we scan beat them. ihollow. That's quite certain, because we can hold them now.

. . . . At the Battle of the Marne we had quite ii small lot of artillery, but still we won in the finish. . . . lam in a very lucky bat- tery; it has been right through, and has lost seventeen men up till now and I am one of the unlucky seventeen. I am wounded, but as soon as I am better I am willing to go back and do my bit ' again.- If the Warrington lads will do the same as me; that is, do their little bit,' the war will be over so much the sooner. I am not a- `big' man, of course, but still I do say that more artillery is badly needed, because the Germans have more guns than we and the French have put together, and bigger guns, too. As soon as we get more artillery we shall-be able to push the Germans on to their own ground." (Cheers.)

===== 0002601_19150227_art0123.txt | included=true | included =====
F LORD DERBY DOES NOT GET HIS. BRIGADE

Loid DERBY then briefly replied on behalf of himself and the other speakers, remarking that he would like to publicly thank the previous speaker for making the best recruiting speech of the evening. (Cheers.) "Alderman Bennett," he added, "has told you about one of my an- cestors who was prepared to burn down your town if he did not get what he wanted. (Laugh- ter.) Well, look out for your town if I don't get my brigade; at any rate, if I don't burn it down I'll make it warm for you." (Laughter and applause.)

ALLEGED ILLTREATMENT OF BRITISH

WOUNDED.

Allegations Against the Germans That Will

Make the. Blood 8011.

Referring to the remarks of Private Pether, his Lordship said: "He is lucky as compared to the wounded men .who fall into German hands. Some of those men have come back now and I can tell you this as a fact, that their statement‘i are being taken down. I happen to have heard privately of something of what they. have suffered, and I toll you that when you hear of the way the Germans have treated these men, and are treating our wounded, there is not a man among you who would- not wish to kill it' German before he died. It will make your blood boil when you hear publicly what I have heard privately." His Lordship then thanked the Mayor for pre- siding, after which the meeting concluded with the singing of the National Anthem: -

WAR ITEMS.

A CORRECTION. Last week we stated that in the list of ' casualties at the Front was the name of a brother of the young man who was recently killed by a circular saw. This was incorrect. The young man in question had no brother.

"P. & K." MEN WITH THE FORCES.

Since the beginning of the war 570 men hi4v joined the colours from Messrs. Pearson and Knowles' works.

COMPLETE!

The second company of the National Reserve, which has been recruited locally, is now com- plete.

CONCERTS FOR THE WOUNDED. On Sunday afternoon the wounded soldiers in the Warrington Infirmary enjoyed yet another • excellent concert, arranged for them by Mr. . W. B. Randall, whilst on Thursday evening several of the artistes appearing at the Hippo- - drome this week dropped in and entertained A. the men.

PREPARATORY TRAINING cORPS:

During the week the drills in connection with the Warrington and District Preparatory Train- ing Corps have been marked by increased attend- ances, a happy state of affairs, which, it is hoped, will continue. Last week-end the corps marched out to Appleton, and, after battalion drill, were put through skirmishing work.

WARRINGTON SOCIETY AND THE WAR.

"The Warrington Society," states the annual report, have in various ways been doing what they could to assist,in the present great crisis . . . and the committee are proud to record the fact that the following have joined the colours: J. Bainbridge, sth (Reserve) Battalion King's Own Lancasters; Sidney Baxter, Che- shire Yeomanry; Harry Dolan, sth (Reserve) Battalion King's Own Lancasters; Philik_Gar- nett, Ist Sportsmen's Battalion, Royal Fusi- liers; Charles D. Law, 4th Battalion South Lancashires; H. Gordon Roberts, 4th Battalion South Lancashires."

FROST-BITTEN WARRINGTON WARRIOR.

Among the soldiers belonging to Warrington who have been in the firing line and have been invalided home is Trooper Fred Kershaw, of 66„ Willis-street, Manchester-road. Trooper Ker- shaw was sent from the battlefield at Ypres to a Southampton Hospital, suffering from frost- bite in his feet. It was at first thought that one - foot would have to be amputated, but fortu- nately such an operation became unnecessary. Trooper Kershaw; who is now 22 years of age and an only son, was formerly a clerk in the employ Of Messrs. Rylands Bros. Having some, military ambition, he joined the Army, • and when the present war broke out was in Egypt, having had three years' service. It was while at Ypres .that he lost his two comrades-in-arms, Troopers Sydney Taylor and George Smith, both of whom were Warringtonians, and one of whom. met his death by shrapnel and the other at the hands of a sniper in an attack on the Prussian Guards. It was through a letter from .Trooper Kershaw that the news of the deaths of these two gallant fellows reached Warrington in Nov- ember last.

AN UPLIFTING HAND.

Helpful Work Amongst the Law-

Breakers.

WHAT THE POLICE COURT

MISSION DOES.

MISSION WORK GAINING GROUND.

Local Horse Breeders' Success.—Mr. James Gould, of Lymm, was successful in winning with his horse, Lymm Thumper, the sixth prize in the class for. yearling stallions at the recent show of the Shire Horse Society. He also won fifth prize in the class for stallions 16.2 hands and over.

Council Not Responsible.—At the monthly meeting of the Urban District Council on Monday night it was decided to endorse the view of the Roads Committee that the Council are not responsible for the fencing at the Ditch- field Bridge, and will not be responsible for any accident that may happeu there owing to its present unfenced condition. The Clerk was in- structed to write to the Manchester Ship Canal Company to that effect.

Boy's Narrow Escape in Accident-4i narrow escape from serious injury was experienced on Thursday, night by Leonard Bradburn, aged seven. As he was crossing the road at Booth's Hill to enter his mothers house, two mctors -74?elonging respectively to 'Mr. Jones (of Hale) and Mrs. Johnson—approached in opposite directions. One of these threw the 15by hack, but, though he escaped the wheels, his head struck the pavement and was cut: open. He Wal4,-intmediately conveyed to the hospital, wherepr.- Thorpe attended -.him. Het is now at home, progressing satisfactorily, ~

MANCHESTER . MEDICAL • CHARITIES.

Lymm Congregations To Be Asked To

Support Them:

" At.the monthlymeeting-of the Urban District Council on Monday evening the Chairman (Mr. T. H. Warburton} appealed-for, pecuniary aid for•the,Mancliester and Salford Medical.`Chari- ties; mentioning- that he had -been' requested to do- so by the governing body of the charities named. Although the Council had -only:inb- -scribed'lss. to the charities in question during the past month, 106 local cases had been treated by them. Of course; he knv that -since the -outbreak Of the. war they had aIP "had their pockets -pulled pretty deeply," htt still he thought that such useful charities as those he had mentioned. ought not to be allowed to suffer unduly for lack of funds, and his main object in mentioning the Matter that evening was to make it knoWn that-he-would feel greatly pleased if each . local church or chapel would arrange, during the coming months, to devote one . Sunday's offerings -in. aid of -.them, at the same- time 'remembering that the Warrington Infirmary Must - not be. neglected." -Still, they did not 4end the nuniber of cases from -their district to 'Warrington. that -they • did to Man- chester; possiblY because, from the medical point of view, Manchester was--supposed' to be one of the finest medical places in the world. A Budding Pianist.--,-At the Widnes .Eisteddfod on Thursday-, Master . Albert Greenough; of .Stoektan-Iteath, won the first prize in ,the pianoforte solo competition for boys And girls under tyi-elve.

Hill Cliffs Girls' Guild.—On Thursday of last week the first Anniversary of the above Guild .was kept. The schoolroom, which had; been „tastefully deCOrated- hY.' the-ladies- of the com- mittee,-was filled with. an appreciative audi- ence.• The entertainment was entirely carried out by the young girl members of the Guild.— The Secretary, Mrs. .Roughsedge, who had :trained the young people, in the singing, in the course of a brief, speech, stated 'that the Guild had had. a successful year's work, and. numbered 6, active members'. ,The girls had physical drill (under the training Of Miss. Dodd), sewing, singing, and also time for amusement, and they seemed to look .forward to their meetings.—. The Rev. G. Curtis was the chairman for the even- ing and Miss Challoner the pianist[' Many the pieces -given .were of a patriotic character, and they were all -greatly applauded. The Manner in which they were perfortaed, reflected great credit on those responsible for training the performers. God save the King" brought a happy evening to a close.

INDUSTRIAL CONTROL.

Mr. A. N. Shimmin Suggests Remedies for an "Inflexible System."

===== 0002601_19150227_art0161.txt | included=true | included =====
EFFECT OF THE WAR.

We are living in the midst of an industrial System which is not nearly as flexible as it should be. On every hand we have the demand for higher wages. We have strikes in progress in all parts of the country, which indicate that the system has not altered when the external facts upon which the living of the people de- pends have altered. Consequently, we get a discrepancy—and a very serious discrepancy. We dread a change, and it is perfectly true to say that we dread elastic systems. But what Strikes us most is: Why have we tolerated so long such an inflexible system, when the whole standard of living demands one which will move in response to the needs of the industrial classes?

With these remarks and this question, Mr. A. N.•Shimmin (of Manchester University, tutor of the hical W.E.A. Tutorial Casa) commenced the eloquent and educative lecture Upon "The War-:11-ndustria1 Control,"- which; under the auipices of the —Workers' Educational Associa- tion, he delivered in :the Cooperativ-e Sankey-street, on Tuesday evening. He had already lectured a-week before upon "The -War and Wages." There was a large attendance, and at th&coOlkipion of .thejeeture Eigiood num- ber of questions referring to points raised by the speaker were answered' by 'him. Councillor E. -Osborne. Occupied thp Chair, ,and there Were also present Messrs. W. H. Sherburn (chair- man):and F. Rennie (hon. secretary).

===== 0002601_19150227_art0162.txt | included=true | included =====
COUNCILLOR OSBORNE AND SOCIALISM IN PRACTICE.

The Chairman said.b.e-th-047.ht that the work of the branch' was not sufficiently appreciated; at any rate, by the people of Warrington. He had attended the previous lecture, and regretted to find that the members of the Town Council and Education Committee had not been present to -hear it. -Everyone who was out, to educate democracy should make a special point of at- tending these and similar lectures. There was an apathy amongst not only the educated Classes, but also, to some extent, amongst the 'working classes in these great matters. To most of them the question of economics was a hard and a dry one, but tri,liciso:vglio were anxious todo what they cotild fCr:the iieople it was abso- lutqy ,necessary that they should know' some- thing about it. They were all agreed that if nily good was to Come it would be by the education of democracy —not only the men who worked, but also the workers who-thought: And if. they could accom- plish that in somelneasure. they would be doing .a great Work. Ile`'had been Ye/7 much 'sin.- Urified4.j.n the little efforts he had put forth in ,gtfa otifyiit4 o public work and coming to dis- *anything's ' which a knowlredge of economics was necessary, to find that to a very great extentit,was a negketedolt,ud.r-' :ffelicPed that, if their friends.did notw attend the lec- tures, they would :reed biiali'and thus obtain information which Would be a considerable ad- vanta6; ' Industrial control,- concluded Councillor Osborne,, was ,a 'question which must appeal to all workers.„ At' any .time it was a very serious subject. Some of them believed4hat the State ought to take:,a keener interest in it, and do -More in that respect. But,llthe moment. they /I.ggested.‘anything practicable, they were con- • fronted by' the statement that it was "Social- ism." and once they gave a person or 'a cause a 'sad name it stuck for ever. (Laughter.) Party government had its names for its own subjects, but .more Socialism was .being: practised to-day linder different. names than ever before. He did not cam what it was ca;led so long as they got something which would be of advantage. (A pplause.) "CANNOT LIVE ON PATRIOTISM."

The -Lecturer said that however much their Patriotism might move them, they still could .not allow it to, overcome the fact- that since ,the beginning of ,the war their wages had decreased. The question was asked ,iv Why do the workers .want to crate* a "disturbance at a critical time like this? It,ia Unpatriotic!" That feeling had characterised the country-fiince the outbreak of *ar. But they could not live on .patriotism, and their patriotism should be.to the Atandard of living of the person. Therefore the worker was _perfectly justified in pointing out that his economic •position was ,gradually approaching starvation. , It was not thissountry or ,another that must be their concern, they lived, primarily as individuals.,

• Nationalisation of Resources. - Was the economic system ;so inflexible that this must occur every time .such a thing took Place? Such an .idea had takent place as , the nationalisation of the workers' resources—put- ting it all together for the .common benefit of the working coinmunity. ,Every dislocation came with the heavieit shock upon just the class which the ,present industrial 'system gave the least power to face, and the system gave them no chance- The realisation, of this fact reflected a, tremendous new social consciousness on-the part of the Government. .But that social consciousness was at present without guidance; it knew something was wrong, but it did not quite realise what; and—worst of did not realise its object! That was- the main fact Which had, up to the present, emerged from the War.

Trade Unionism's Mistake.

, ;The Lecturer .then-recouuted the history of the growth of Capitalism,' and described how it had -entered.; and influenced . politics until the worker. was :not represented- in government .as he_ ought to be; Thus this social consciousness Was immensely significant,.Luid-encouraged them to hope that. state of things-would not always he so. He, however, strongly deprecated .the assumption—running on either side—that ,„,Uapital and Labour.would-he for ever opposed. v ' rade.unionism had,. tended to emphasise that there was a- lasting, division 'between the. two; but .he thought '(with , all ,due respect-to the good- sides of trade unionism) that that was one 0-,its bad sides, and one that ecruld well be done away with. He firmly believed. 'that. it

was not iossible to combine the economic and nolitical, interests through one channel—or at least through one imachine—in . the form of government. Therefore the two functions ought to be separated. They had been worked for so Many years along one line that the result was ?Illy to be expected. The whole fault was to hc .I,Aid at the door of the system...

NORTH CHESHIRE LEAGUE.

STOCKTON HEATH V. BROADHEATH LADS

These teams met on the West-a,yenue ground. Neither side was fully represented, the visitors completing, their team with substitutes.. The "Heathens" won the toss and played .with the sun at their backs. From the kick-off the home forwards seeured. possession and raced to, the visitors' end, and after ,several efforts- had been successfully repulsed, good „play- on the home right wing resulted in. Lloyd-and Smith leading a strong attack on the visitors' goal. Marsh secured from a clearance and opened the score after .five. minutes'„ play. Following this the game was evenly„contested until near .the inter- val, when the visitors' defence wasihard pressed and, the backs made several fine clearances. Marsh succeeded in scoring a second goal with a long drive. Half-time: Stockton Heath, 2 goals; ,Broadheath, nil. • The home team were early in evidence on re- -suming, with strong play and the visitors' defenders had again to submit to defeat, Marsh doing the " hat trick." Continuing to have the best of the. game, Smith quickly registered the -fourth goal.. Following these reverses, the visitors improved in. their play and on several occasions threatened the home goal, but the defence proved equal to the attack and drove them back. The visitors' goal was again in danger, when the centre-half cleared by good judgment and gave his forwards possession, and the ball being sent to the inside-idt.-.he beat several opponents and finally 1;ot past Hooper with a, splendid shot. After this the players for a while slackened their efforts, but as the end was approaching the -"Heathens" again asserted themselves 'and . good- play by Noden and „Bentley 'resulted in the. latter scoring the fifth goal. Besult:—Stockton Heath; 5 goals; Broadheath Lads, 1 goal.

===== 0002601_19150227_art0207.txt | included=true | included =====
PADGATE.

Wesley Guild.—" A Talk about Tolstoi " was the subject of a very interesting lecture given by Mr. J. Gauld, of Stretton, at the Wesley Guild meeting on Monday evening. The lecturer gave a brief description of Tolstoi's early life, his short university career, and told how afterwards he became famous as an author. He described the character of several of the books written by this eminent author, but specially referred to "Resurrection." He explained how this book was objected to by many of the English read- ing public as being an immoral book, but said that Tolstoi's idea was for the uplifting of man- kind. The usual vote of thanks was moved by Mr. 'W. E. Plinston, and seconded by Dr. Ben- nett. The Rev. A, j. Morton. presided. Church Meeting.—The annual church meet- ing was Ileld on Tuesday evening and was pre- sided over by, the Rev. A. J. Morton. Alder- man Bennett presented the financial statement for the year, which showed a satisfactory bal- ance. Several suggestions were made as to ways and means of keeping up the finances of the church, after which a solo was given by

Mrs. Cottrell. The Rev. A. J. Morton delighted the audience with his reading of a humorous selection from Mark Twain. Refreshments were provided.

===== 0002601_19150227_art0208.txt | included=true | included =====
PENKETH.

Wesley .Guild.—On Tuesday a miscellaneous concert was given by members of the Warring- ton Wesleyan Cycling Club in the Wesleyan Schoolroom, before a fair audience, who thor- oughly appreciated the efforts of the follow- ing artistes: Solos by Miss Ward, Miss Ker- shaw, and Mrs. •Plinston; duets by Misses M. and V. Jones, Miss Turner, and Mr. White; violin solo (single string), Mr., L, Lawton; read- ings and , recitations by Mr. G. Whitfield and Mr. C. Wilcockson.

actually possible to go to them and say, "Look what we have saved you!" and not "Look what we have cost you The charge for the Catho- lic children in the proposed schools could not be more than is. each a. week, whilst the cost

GOOD FOR ONE, GOOD FOR ALL.

Mr. G. Mather said that if it was good for the Roman Catholic children to have the special religious instruction, and the special fatherly and motherly care that had been talked about, the children of other denominations should be given exactly the same privileges. Mr: Shaw: Promote it. then!

Mr. Flanagan said that the Catholics on the Board would offer no objection were the pro- posal made to ,send the Church children to, special Church liomes; indeed, they would be among the first to say,. "Send them to any. place you like that you think will be better for them!" Mr. Bennett was in favour of the children going to the school', and thought in the long run it would be found to save fife Board from heavy cost in the shape of new buildings.

===== 0002601_19150227_art0210.txt | included=true | included =====
BROAD-MINDED MEMBERS.

Mr. F. McKinley said he was glad that they had such broad-minded gentlemen on the Board as the Rev. A. M. Mitchell, and hoped the 'Board as a whole would be broad-minded enough to see the justice of the application from the Catho- lics, for he was sure that the sending qf the Roman Catholic children to the special schools would in the long run be found to work out to the benefit of all concerned.

Mr. J. Backhouse favoured •ilie preparation of a report showing what effect the removal of the Catholic children would have on the adminis- tration and cost of the Padgate Homes.

===== 0002601_19150227_art0211.txt | included=true | included =====
ARE ROMAN CATHOLICS DOING THEIR DUTY?

• The Chairman said that nnfortunately the religious side of the question had been some- what emphasised by the advocates of the tram- ference of the children. In committee the pre- vious day it was distinctly stated that the Church of England and the Nonconformist bodies should undertake their dutieS to their children, whereupon Mr. Brooks told them that the Catholic population in Warrington was about one-fifth of the total, whilst later on he told them that of the children dependent upon the Guardians about two-fifths were Catholics. In face of those figures, he did not think it was either fair or reasonable that the suggestion should have been made that other religions had neglected their duties, for if the Catholics pro- vided in the same ratio for their dependent children as did the Church of England and the Nonconformist bodies, the number under the Guardians would be about half of what it was. It was said that the change was one which could be justly advocated on economic lines, but he disputed 'that, and went on to contend that it was the duty of the Guardians to look after, the children who became chargeable to the Poor Law, without any regard to sect or creed, or any consideration of that kind. They should all be treated equally well; none should be penalised and none should be favoured. What the Board were being asked to do that day was to favour certain denominational schools by sending certain children there and paying a price at which the management of the schools in question could make a small profit. It should not be suggested that those s`clools would be conferring a favour on the Board. It was simply nonsense for anybody to get up and say that the managers of those Roman Catholic schools could treat their children better than they were treated in the best Poor Law schools, and at less cost. The treatment that the Board had given their, poor children, were giving them, and proposed to give them, was better than the treatment they could have in any special schools of the kind referred to that day. They ought to deal with the children absolutely, irre- spective of creed or denomination, and ought not to .:übsidise denoniT ational institutions out of public funds, which was what they were being asked to do that morning.

===== 0002601_19150227_art0212.txt | included=true | included =====

Upon a vote being taken, there voted for the amendment: The. Chairman and Messrs. J. ackhouse, E. L. Bent, T. Dale, W. Furness, R. Lee, P. Longton and G. Matt er• and against it: Mrs. A. Hall, Miss Broadbent, the Rev. A. M. Mitchell, and Messrs. J. W. Knowles, W. C. Brooks, J. Cook, W. Flanagan, G. Allen, J. A. Hughes, F. McKinley, Dennett. T. Ritchie, W. J. Shaw and M. W. Seton. A.

WAKAINUTON SOCIETY.

Alderman Bennett Becomes its Second President.

Alderman Arthur Bennett was unanimously elected president Of the Warrington Society— in place of the late Mr. Wm. 'Long, who was the first president—at the annual meeting of that body held in the Old Academy on Thurs.:- day evening. In returning thanks for his election he said that since the recent death of Mr. Wm. Long, who was their first, and only, president, he had been thinking about the appointment of his successor, and his mind had been running on the names of prominent men outside the Society, but with close local connections, and who might presumably be interested in the work of the Society and able and willing to help—such men as Lord Lilford, Mr. Robert Pierpoint, and Mr. J. Rylands, S:c. Therefore he was greatly surprised when he found that he had been nominated for the position. .He could scarcely say how much he appreciated the compliment, for he regarded the presidency of such a society as that was a very high honour indeed. He thought he could claim to take the deepest possible interest in the affairs of the Society. Queen Mary was supposed to have said that when she died the word Calais would be written on her heart. Well, if he were to die they would probably find the Old Academy written on his, for every britk and stone in the building was dear to him, and no incident in all his public life had given him greater satis- faction than the part he was privileged to play in helping to save the building from destruc- tion and to preserve it for a noble and appro- priate use. Other officers elected were: Hon. treasurer, Mr. J. Foster Jackson; hon. secretaries, Messrs. Jas. Richardson and H. S. Page (the latter being appointed to succeed Alderman Bennett upon his elevation to the presidency); hon. auditors, Mr. John Fairhurst; committee, Dr. Peacocke, and Messrs. J. W. Bibby, L. Green- ing, H. Stuart rage, John Fairhurst, C. Maw- son, W. Richardson, E. Parry, Thos. Cooper, A. Irotherton, J. Longland, G. Brook and A. T. Hallaway. Earlier in the evening Alderman Bennett sub- mitted the annual report, which was adopted. It showed that nine new members had been elected since the last annual meeting and that there were now 123 names on the books, as com- pared with 125 last year.

HOW TO ADDRESS "TOMMY'S" LETTERS.

The Town Clerk frequently receives inquiries from soldiers' wives and other relatives as to the amounts chargeable for forwarding letters and parcels .to their men folk in the fighting line, and asks us to assist in making the rates known, in the hope that thereby both the rela- tives and himself may be saved the trouble of letter writing. Letters for the front must bear stamps at the rate of Id. for every ounce, and the charges for forwarding the parcels to mem- bers of the expeditionary force are as follows: If weighing not more than 31b., Is.; if weighing over 31b. and not, more than 71b., Is,. 4d.; if weighing over 71b. and not. more than 111 b., is. 71. Newspapers go at the ordinary 'home rates (id.), provided they do not weigh more than 2oz.

TWENTY-FIVE DAYS IN THE TRENCHES.

Warrington " Tommy " Writes From A Place "Littered With German Dead."

"We have just come out of the trenches for a short rest, but will be back again in the thick of it by the time you receive this," writes Pte. Edward Linaker, of the King's Liverpool Regi- ment, to his cousin, Mrs. T. Jones, of 96, School Brow, Warrington.

"lam still alive and getting on as well as can be expected," he adds. "It is just over six months since I arrived out here [France l with my regiment, and I can assure you that we have had 'some hard fighting. I am pleased to say that I have come safely through all the en- gagements my regiment has been in up to the time of writing, and they include Monti, the Marne, the Aisne, Ypres and La Bassee. . . . I think the hardest time we had was at Ypres, where We were in the trenches for twenty-five days, and that without a wash. The Germans greatly outnumbered us there, but we managed to keep them back, though it took some hard fighting, and in spite of the fact that they brought their famous Prussian Guards against us.

===== 0002601_19150227_art0215.txt | included=true | included =====
Repulsed the Famous Prussian Guards.

One.morning I shall never forget they made a particularly desperate charge at us, but they went back quicker than they came. The place we are in at present is simply littered with dead Germans. On the 25th of last month they made a determined onslaught on us, their idea being to give the Kaiser a birthday present—his birth- day was two days later—but we knocked them over like skittles. The counttyside around here is in an awful state. Everything is in ruins— churches, public buildings, houses; there is not a good one left standing.

===== 0002601_19150227_art0216.txt | included=true | included =====
The Unscathed. Sacred _lmage.

The Germans seem to be particularly fond of shelling ‘.churches. Near here, th.) tg-n_ the church itself is blown practically away, the image of the Virgin Mary still stands, and their shells go round ,and. about it every day, but without harming it. . . . Whilst lam writ- ing this -fetter the,. German artillery is shelling out position. . . . I hear that our "Bill" is in hospital at Birkenhead with 'frozen feet."

THE LATE MR. WM. LONG.

Memorial Service At Cairo-Street

Chapel.

A service in memory of the late Mr. William Long, of Grappenhall and of Cleabarrow, Win- dermere, was conducted in the Caird-street Uni- tarian Chapel, 'Warrington, on Sunday morn- in F by the Rev. W. H. Drummond, of London, 'who, in the'co'urse of his address to the'goodly- sized congregation, made eulogistic reference to the life and character of the deceased gentle- man.

Taking as his text the words "I stir up your (-pure blind by way, of -remembrance," the preacher said that the late Mr. Long treasured the memories of the past and helped to keep them alive in these bustling days when they were all too liable to forget. William Long was endowed with that quality to which the Romans so often referred—that quality which flmbraced not only piety towards God, but also loyalty to family traditions and public memories, lie was proud of the Liberal Nonconformity in which he ha-d been born and trained; he followed its worship and influences in his life. It was not the constraint of formal duty, but a real sense of help and renewal of heart which drew him away from the crowd into the small company, which, Sunday. by Sunday, gathered for wor- ship in that building. It was also an act of loyalty to a tradition which- he believed it was important to keep alive in personal and national life.

===== 0002601_19150227_art0218.txt | included=true | included =====
On Equal terms With All Men.

It was the positive sign of Nonconformity Which he valued., not its negations and dis- putements. Of William Long's respect for manhood it was unnecessary to speak, save, perhaps, to say that he had an unusual gift for being on equal terms with all sorts and condi- tions of men.- There was never a trace of con- descension in his kindness. He (the preacher) would refer to one public service which William Long performed—a service which was at once an act of friendly homage to the memories of Cairo- street chapel and to Warrington as a whole. It was due entirely to his energy and enthusi- asm that a window was placed in the library of Manchester College, Oxford, in order to com- memorate the worthies of Warrington Academy —Priestley, the Aikens, Gilbert Wakefield, and the other great men of Liberal Nonconformist tradition. William Long spared no trouble to make that memorial what it ought to be. He scrutinised closely every detail of the design, and the day on which the 'Window was unveiled was one of the greatest days of his life.

ONE OF THE FOUNDERS or THE CHAMBER OF COMMERCE.

At the annual meeting of the Warrington Chamber of Commerce, held in the Town. Van on Monday morning; the Secretary (Alderman Arthur Bennett) made reference to the death oT Mr. Long, remarking that that gentleman wak one of the founders of the Chamber, was its president in 1884 and 1885, and, for many years, 'took an active part in its work. The Vice-President of the Chamber (Mr. J. J. Whitley)—who, in the absence, Of the President , (Major Crosfield) took the chair—proposed a vote of heartfelt sympathy with Mrs. Long and family in their sad bereavement. Mr. F. A. Frost seconded, remarking that he had known the late Mr. Long for many years, and had always found him keen or doing his best for the town as a whole, for its commerce, and for the integrity of business Principles. (Hear, hear.) His death was a great loss to the community. The Secretary said that his connection with the late Mr. Long dated back •to the days— longer ago than he cared to remember—when he was an , articled pupil, when as, indeed, always, he found the deceased, gentleman most kind and helpful. For many years Mr. Long was one of the most active members of that Chamber, and the, gap which had been caused by his demise was one that it would be very difficult to fill. (Hear, hear.) The vote was carried in silence, all standing as a mark of respect.

WARRINGTON SOCIETY'S CONDOLENCES. At the sixteenth annual meeting of the War- rington Society, held at the Old Academy on Thursday evening, Mr. 11. Stuart Page proposed a vote of condolence with the widow and family of the late Mr. Lobg, who was the Society's first and only president up to the time of his d,eath. The Society, said Mr. Page, owed the deceased gentleman a great deal for its present. flourish- ing condition; indeed, it was only the older members who would adequately realise how great was the Society's indebtedness to Mr. Long. When the fate of the Old 'Academy hung in the balance, and there were those who thought that the cash value of the site was of more worth to the town than the associations which clung aroun 1 that ancient building—a building which connected the town with some lf the greatest thinkers of the eighteenth cen- tury—Mr. Long very generously expressed .his willingness to purchase the site, if necessary, and thus save the building from destruction. However, that was not necessary, for better counsels prevailed with the powers that were; and the building was allowed to stand and was handed over to the care of the Warrington, Society. Alderman Bennett, who has been appointed to succeed Mr. Long in the presidency of the Society, seconded; observing that it was prob- able that but for the help of the late President, whose demise they all mourned, that the Society would never have existed, and certainly he was one of its most generous supporters, and during the whole period of the Society's existence the members had been given abundant proof of his kindness and of his interest in their work; as also of the kindness and interest of his wife. Mr. Long had passed away, but his memory remained with them, a treasured recollection. __

The proposition was carried in silence, all standing.

WARRINGTON GUARDIANS SAY

"YES."

The qneStion as to whether or not the Roman Catholic Poch. Law, 'Children at present quar- tered in the Padgate Cottage Homes shall be transferred .to special Catholic schools was debated at considerable length at the fort- nightly meeting Of the Warrington Board of Guardians, held at the Workhouse, under the chairmanship of Mr. A. J. Jolley, on Wednesday morning. The discussion arose on the following report presented by the Homes Committee :—" The committee further considered the letters for- 'Warded by the Rev. Father Pennington and the Rev: Father Whittle relative to the proposal of the Guardians to erect new buildings at the Cottage Holies to accommodate certain classes of children under the provisions• of the Poor • Law Institution Order, 1913, and urging the Guardians to take into consideration, as an alternative to the building proposals, the ques- tion of sending the Roman-Catholic children to certified schools under the control of the Roman Catholic Archbishop of Liverpool. It was moved by Mr. Brooks and seconded by Mr. Seton that the committee recommend that the Roman Catholic children in the Cottage Homes be sent to certified Roman Catholic schools. There voted: For, 11; against, 7; and the Chair- man declared the motion carried."

===== 0002601_19150227_art0221.txt | included=true | included =====
AN AMENDMENT TO REFER THE MATTER BACK.

Mr.- R. Lee said he must enter his protest against the Roman Catholic children being; sent to other Homes. He thought the Guar- dians should keep the children under their own supervision. Mr. T. Dale moved as an amendment that the minute of the committee be referred back for furthcr consideration, believing as he did that a matter of such vast importance to the Homes ought to have the very fullest possible discus- sion and should not be rushed. Mr. W. Furness seconded. Had there been no accommodation in the Homes for the 63 Roman Catholic children he would, he said, have been the first to support the resolution, but, as a matter of fact, there was adequate accommodation and provision at Padgate for these children; indeed, there was room for many more children than were actually there, whilst the Homes were so adequately staffed that there was really not sufficient work to keep the whole of the staff fully occupied. That being so, it seemed to him, as a business man, that it would be almost suicidal, and certainly not a financially sound thing, to send the 63 Catholic children away to other Homes, at a cost of something like .2900 a year to the Board. If they were prepared to consider a scheme not only for sending the Catholic children to Catholic Homes, but for, also, giving the Pro- testant children a chance of being boarded-out, and thus dispensing with the Homes altogether, he would not object. But the present proposal seemed to him unreasonable and he certainly thought the proper course would be to adjourn the matter for more careful and fuller consid- eration. It was a most important matter and -one that should not be rushed. The best thing, in his opinion, as well as the most economical, would be to make arrangements for the chil- dren in the Homes to attend local .chools, and then Mrs. Lawrence could remain in charge of the Homes, and it would be better for the children themselves.

===== 0002601_19150227_art0222.txt | included=true | included =====
MIGHT OBVIATE NEW BUILDING OPERATIONS.

Miss Broad Vent said that it was hoped that the carrying out of the recommendation put forward by the Homes Committee would ob- viate. the necessity of providing new buildings for the accommodation of the children over three years of age who, under a new regulation, would have to leave the Workhouse on April Ist next. The Board would have to make pro- vision for those children, and the committee considered that sending the Catholic children to these special Homes would be the most economical way of doing it. The whole point of the matter was that the Rotuan. Catholics wished their children to be brought up in the atmosphere of Roman Catholicism. (Hear, hear.)

===== 0002601_19150227_art0223.txt | included=true | included =====
CATHOLIC TRAINING FOR CATHOLIC CHILDREN. ,

blr. J. W. Knowles thought there was not much in the suggestion that the children from the Homes should be sent to local schools; at least, so far as the Catholic children were concerned, for the nearest Catholic school to Pailgate was at Woolston, two miles away. He was a Low Churchman personally, thrugh he was sorry to say that his religion had very nearly gone—(laughter)—but he had a strong feeling that these particular Roman Catholic children should have the opportunity of being brought up amid the same religious rigour as other Roman Catholic children were. They had got a religion and must be taught in that religion, and what Roman Catholicism could be imparted 'to them by the R,omish priest, who was paid a trifle of £lO or £2O a year to go and see them once a week, could not be very much. The Roman Catholics were most religious people, and, rigorously brought up themselves, they believed that their children should be rigorously brought up also.

===== 0002601_19150227_art0224.txt | included=true | included =====
FATHERLY AND MOTHERLY CARE.

The,Rev. A. M. Mitchell pointed out that the sum of .000 a year mentioned by Mr. Furness as the cost of sending the children to the Catholic schools would not be extra outlay, for it must cost almost as much, if not more, to keep the children in the Padgate Homes at the present time. , What the committee pro- posed to do was simply an enlargement of some- thing that had been already done, for the Board already had some children in Catholic schools, and, if they considered that the smaller number were already being well eared for in those schools, he could see no reason why they should hesitate to hand over the larger number to the same kind of fatherly and motherly care.

===== 0002601_19150227_art0225.txt | included=true | included =====
Done By Other Unions.

Moreover, what was proposed was already dope by all the other Unions in this part of 'Lancashire, and why should the Warrington Union ,make itself remarkable by standing out in the way it had been doing? What had been found so very satisfactory in the case of other Unions would surely prove equally satisfactory in the, case of Warrington. lie bad seen a let- ter in which it was made clear that the care taken of the children in the Roman Catholic schools was all that could be •desired; in fact, he drew the inference that it could not be im- proved upon, and, consequently, he thought they need have no hesitation whatever in band- ing over the children. The Catholics, in his judgment, were quite right in the request they had made. The Romish Church was very strong on pel'sonal influence and personal con- tact, and in the importance_ of a tone and an atmosphere, and it was ridiculous to think 'that those things could be imparted in the short space of one hour's teaching per week. He would like to see that distinctive tone and at- mosphere introduced also in connection with Church of England and Nonconformist chil- dren. It had been suggested. that buildings such as the Padgate Homes might. be required very shortly for military hospitals; indeed, that they might be commandeered' for the pur- poses of the wounded soldiers, and that being so it would be well for the Board to be pre- pared supposing the order should come down. Mr. E. L. Bent said that whilst he was! in favour of something along the lines of the corn- mittee's suggestion, he could see no harm in referring the matter back for further considera- tion.

===== 0002601_19150227_art0227.txt | included=true | included =====
"TIPPERARY."

Some Comments on the Remarkable - Taste of Tommy Atkins.

There are songs that roll into favour with the roll of the drum, and most of them have an odd history. -• Rouget de l'lsle wrote the most famous of them all to greet .the passing .Army of the Marseillaise on its way through Stras- burg. He was a lieutenant there, and—so one of the stories goes—he was tipsy. when he wrote it. He finished the lines and music hastily, drowsily, to meet the coming of the " black- browed mass" Of revolutionists at dawn. With the last note dotted upon the paper, his head toppled into his hands and he fell asleep. The sheet that Waited for the opening of his eyes contained the " Marseillaiset"—" the greatest war song ever promulgated," of which Carlyle said: "Whole armies will sing it, with eyes weeping and burning, with hearts defiant of death; despot, and devil,'!' The song in which the German soldier' finds his Courage,' 'The Watch of the Rhine," was written by. Max Schneckenburger, and crept into a provincial newspaper. In the most 'stirring days that America has known, sothebody began to sing John BrOivn's Body," and hever since the march of Cromwell's Ironsides to the Psalm "Let God arise, let His enemies he, scattered," have men gone with more cOnfident stride through' mud and storm than those who went to their death singing Of the body 0,4 lay "a -mouldering in the grave." • But "John Brown." was real poetry when compared with the rollicking jargon and Stir- ring nonsense of "Marching through Georgia." And the American soldiers in the Spanish War, with all the world of melody to choose. from, decided upon "There'll be a .hot Time in the Old Town To-night." . All of us, it would appear, have something to learn concerning a soldier's choice of a ditty. The knowing song writer will be upon us with an improved aphorism, "Let me write the songs of. an army and I will let the other side use dum-dum bullets." "What inventor," he will ask, "devising ingenious ways. of killing men, ever advanced a cause so much as he who wrote the doggerel of 'Yankee Doodle'?"

"TIPPERARY." 13ef.ore we ,try to aeeount for "Tipperary," let us take a look at it:

Up to mighty London came an Irishman one day, As the streets are paved with gold, sure ev'ryone was gay; Singing- songs of Piccadilly, Strand and Leices- ter Square, Till Paddy got excited; then he shouted to them there: CHORUS-. It's a long- way to, Tipperary, It's a long way to go; It's a long way to Tipperary, To the sweetest girl I know, Good-bye Piccadilly, 'farewell Leicester Square, It's a-long way to Tipperary, But my heart's right there.

Paddy wrote a letter to his Irish Molly 0, Saying, Should you not -reteive it, write and

lot me know; If I make mistakes in spelling, Molly dear,"

said he, "Remember; it's the pen that's had, don't lay the blame on me."

Molly wrote a neat reply to Irish Paddy 0, Saying, "Mike ,Maloney wants to marry me,

and so • Leave the Strand and Piccadilly, or you'll be

to blame, FO love has fairly drove me silly, Hoping you're the same."

Nobody is likely to be offered the Laureate- ship for stuff like that. There is a rhythm and melody and a touch of sentiment. It is supposed to be Irish, but that is quite incidental. Tip- perary means home and love. That is all., It Miglit as well have been Wigan or Manchester if those had been names with a lilt, and a bit of Irish or some other kind of sentiment. That "Tommy Atkins" should choose it as his National song puts us all to the blush. The critics have given him up, and philosophers have had to come to the rescue. :Here speaks one of the best of .them in " T.P.'s Weekly " : ' "All sorts of incalculable things have frus- trated the perfect working of German machine- culture. Not least the curious fact of our men singing 'Tipperary' in the trenches . . . You can do almost anything to a man without destroying his essential splendour—you can beat him and bully him, starve him and cheat him, laugh at him, and even kill him, and yet lie remains sublime, and, on the whole, indifferent to fate, But once you insist upon him acting logically You destroy him utterly. So it is that you will find 'men kindling trivial and some- times ridiculous words at the fire of emotion, and setting up a blaze which shows them the way to victory and honour. It is quite a chance that our soldieis sing 'Tipperary,' two years ago they would have sung with equal gusto 'We all go the same way home.' . . In the ordinary course of things ' Tipperary ' would have passed into the limbo of forgotten popular airs, but 'Mr. Thomas Atkins' has done for it what its author could never • have done; he has given it immortality." TERRORS OF LIGHT-HEARTEDNESS.

Those who are clever enough to Understand all this will understand it. With the closing sentence everybody will agree. But why " Tip- perary " among the multitude of available ditties? Does genius gleam through its stanzas, or was it its lucky star? Certainly there seems nothing propitious about its birth. It was written in New York three years ago by Mr. Harry Williams, and designed for a little vaudeville skit. Those who want the exact place of its nativity will find it at Douglas Manor, Long Island. It was offered' to publish- ers in America without acceptance. Then an English firm gave it printed form, without find- ing that anybody took a special interest in it. But. when marching became the order of the day somebody discovered that " Tipperary " had all the requisites of a war song. It was found to be stimulating and soul-satisfying. Satisfying? Yes; but only to a Britisher. Who, by any stretch of fancy, can hear a Frenchman using such a ditty? It is the choice of men who take a song or a fight as things that fill up the day; and one is taken as lightly as the other. They may be unmusical, but they don't know it; they may be beaten, but they don't know it. They sing and fight till someone tells them that the job is over. Of such glorious thoughtlessness Britain is the abiding home. It cropped 'up at Waterloo as it had cropped up in Elizabeth's spacious day. Perhaps the Kaiser would be more worried than he is if he knew the terrors of British light-heartedness. It is paradoxical in the last degree that the soldier who finds his balm in " Tipperary " will be the'mOst formidable of all.

Alas, it is the balm of sound and not of sense. True, there is something that touches sentiment in the thought of a long, long dis- tant hoine: and " Tipperary " may mean a cot- tage and a garden, a mother that keeps fowls, and bees maybe. But the real comfort is in the long "a" of Tipperary. Like tho.blessecl. Biblical word "Mesopotamia," that brouglA benediction to a certain dear old lady, " Tip- perary " has been found to be a soul-soother. There will be no danger of spoiling the song, so long as that glorious sound is left. The last few discoverable fragments of sense may safely be removed, and Piccadilly and Leicester Square given to the censor. Tipperary .will still be a haven of refuge, and soldier voices will linger in delicious anticipa- tion on the "er " before coming down with a crash on the best vowel in the language. It is part of "Tommy's" fitness that he is not a critic. Good music is good music if he likes it, and not otherwise. "The Watch on the Rhine" may .be tasty, and the " Marseillaise " a stimulant; "God save. the King" is largely advertised; but for a solid meal give him "Tipperary." m.

RAILWAY CLERKS & WAR.

Their Association Prepares to

Meet Trouble

IN THE NEAR FUTURE.

An excellent attendance marked a meeting of the Warrington branch of the Railway Clerks' Association, held on Saturday evening, in the Wtnmarleigh Cafe. Mr. G. Merrick (chairman, of the branch) presided,, and 'a feature of the evening- was an address 'upon the work of the Association, given by Mr. A. E. Townend, of Manchester.

NATIONALISATION OR TRUSTIFICATION? The speaker prefaced his address with an ex- pression of thanks to the Warrington branch*. for having done him the high honour of electing-. him their representative upon the Association executive, lie assured them that whilst enjoy- ing the privileges of the position he would not be unmindful of its duties. There were one or two phases in connection with their work which had developed and affected railway clerks very vitally—especially during the war. These he wanted to deal with from the trade union point of view, demonstrating the usefulness, -ever, essential nature, of the R.C.A. Never had the, need for such an Association been so strikingly illustrated as on that surprising August 4th, when within one hour they all became Civil, servants. Those questions which had been troubling them were then relegated to their proper positions. Everything could be ar- ranged regarding the clerks, when first the prin- cipal question had been decided upon between• their Association and the Government.

On August 22nd they found that all the 'tread' offices were making out what they termed redundant lists—lists a all the men whom they thought would not he wanted at some future time. Let the members not imagine, though,. that after the war they were going back to the old state. It would be either nationalisation-Or trustification. And owing to the influence rail- way magnets had been using in Government circles, the way in which the railways had been run during the last few Months had given the public a very bad opinion of how they would be run permanently by the people's representa- tives. They could rest assured that truatifica- tion would come about. Th,?refore the redund- ant lists would become particularly significant! (Hear, hear) The type of mind which con- trolled the railways was not going to lose on men with wham it cbuld dispense.

===== 0002601_19150227_art0228.txt | included=true | included =====
Superannuation In Danger.

Again, they had thought the superannuation fund quite secure, whethet anything else 'was or not. Yet this was not so! Supposing the railway went backwards; the debenture and ordinary shareholders would come before the clerks, and not afterwards, as they had thought. Well, they were not going to leave things like that; and in the meantime It was up to them to 'strengthen the Association.

===== 0002601_19150227_art0229.txt | included=true | included =====
The Bonus.

To give them a. little encouragement; he would say 'a -word or two about the bonus. (Laughter.) None had done more to help the Government than had the railway clerks, and so they asked for a little assistance in beating' the increased prices of commodities. That morning they heard that 3s. would be given to. those earning under 30s. weekly, and 2s. to those with a wage over 30s. Well; that was a "wee bit." (Laughter.) At any rate, the principle. had been admitted.

They talked about railways, bit it was net so. much railways that concerned them. Trade. unions were not even the end of things by any means; they were only in existence to give a man the opportunity of living. But as mem- bers of society. they gave their best, and it was but fair and logical that they should receive. the best in return. (Applause.) The Chairman moved a hearty vote Of thanks. to Mr. Townend for his address. Mr. Ilulton seconding, the motion was carried unanimously, and the speaker suitably replied.

• HINTS AND INTERESTING FACTS AND CHAT FOR AMATEURS. .....-., , MALE REQUIREMENTS. For egg production only those cocks or cockerels should be selected' that have come from a heavy egg-laying female. There is no doubt but that fecundity 18 'through ; the male side, and, therefore, the best male that can be secured in this direction the better. I know that some of the heaviest egg-laying strains thitt have been.produced have been got by following .thiS.rule...':The influence of such a bird-on the progenYAS enorinout,' They, too, .should possess all theipreed characteristics as in the 'ease of the hens:', The,speeial standard for the male should be stildiedttieeause with certain breeds it varies consiarOblY f tom the stindara , ' SPECIAL POINTS TO REMEMBER. No matter whether pullets or hens are being used as breeders it is important to see that each individual bird is healthy, and has, moreover, a clean record in this direction. Any bird that has suffered from Tulip, for instance, should be Olicarded 4 once. There is always the possi-.. lailitY-.-that such a bird Ma: y transmit to its pro- geny the tendency to contract such a complaint. The same may be said of liver disea ' and kindred ailments. Owing to the war and the greater number of the Recit:' players joining the colours, the popu- =lar club known -as---the--Bank-Quay. .Recti. found it impossible to carry on and 'fulfil their fixtures, and reluctantly had to.'resign.their membership of the League. After this had been done their Honorary 'Secretary (W. Appleton) joined the South Lancashire. UNFORTUNATE ACCIDENT TO MELLOR, OF HOLLINS GREEN. During the progress of the cup-tie at Pad- dington on Saturday,. Mellor, of Hollins Green, sustained- a 'heavy charge, of -which at the time he took_ no notice.. He continued to play, but on hi S -arrival at home het complained of pain at his shoulder and was perinadecl to go to the surgery and be examined.. ' He- was, unfortu- nately, found to be 'suffering from a fractured collar-bone, and this will probably necessitate him being unable to take any- further part in the game this se,ason, and the loss of his ser- vices will be a serious, blow to.hii ,club as they have still a few important engagements to fulfil. , TEAMS FOR - TO-DAY. : • • • - •. . THE CIVIL WAR. When Royalist and Roundhead Fought at Stockton Heath. An interesting and educational lecturette by the .Borough Librarian -.(Mr. Madeley) on -".Cromv-ellian Warrington" was the feature of Sunday's gathering at the Sankey, Bridges Adult :School. The history. .of the. Civil War, the Lecturer said, was somewhat ,confusing, but Warrington was the 'key to the North-West of England, and in 1641 the Earl of Derby made the-town -his-headquarters. In 164.1 the Earl sdyii.,fittacked, but defeated the enemy at Stcick- lOn'lleOth... -After the end of the Ci ii War, in 1646, and .-the-iirrest of the King in 164.7, England was in- vaded by the--Scots, and 'on Monday, August 21st, .1648, the:Sheriff of Chester wrote to Oliver Cromwell desiring -to' know how they might serve 'him, and in what condition Warrington was,. „from Warrington,' Cromwell wrote to the Speaker of the Commons an. account of the pre- vious three days' doings.' • •A Thousand Royalists Slain Near Winwick. 'The Duke of namilton's Army being driven Trom:PreSton, -was' pursted and tittacked again. at Red Bank—between Newton and Winwick— where about a thousand Royalists were killed and 2,ooo'prisoners taken. Following the braen 'Royalists on to- Warrington, -the' Cromwellion troops so 'harassed the enemy that General Bailey, with 4,000 Foot, surrendered whilst the Duke escaped, with his. Cavalry, into Cheshire. If I had but a thousand horse," wrote Crom- well, not, doubt but to give a yell- -good account of them.?' Cromwell's. position , was thus made-clear enough, -but 'the condition, of Warrington at that time-260 years ago— was not such a simple question. The town was not then of a size to be influential,, and it was, rather its geographical position which gave it .importance -;' .it .being the only point where' the .Mersey could be safely 'crossed. f-A•riumber of questions were asked, to which 'Mr.4-Modeler 'replied.
